# Ideas: trends and free sources for Build-It (the hourly Higgsfield runner appends dated, sourced findings)
# Only new things, a few lines per run, newest at the bottom. Check a licence on the site itself before using a source.

## 2026-11-23 21:55 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)
Trends
- Realism over fantasy: TikTok's 2026 trend report says viewers are moving "away from fantasy toward realism", with
  curiosity-driven discovery. Fits the photoreal Seedance look: keep every shot believable, no fantasy glow.
  [socialpilot, upd. Nov 17 2026](https://www.socialpilot.co/blog/tiktok-trends)
- Then-and-now from the same spot, years apart, hard cut (trending on Pink Floyd "Time", ~556K videos). Build-It use:
  a B5 pair from one camera angle (Golden Gate 1937 vs today, Kuwait Towers 1979 vs today); 85 Dubai Frame (old vs
  new Dubai) is built for it. [socialpilot](https://www.socialpilot.co/blog/tiktok-trends)
- Tiny text that zooms into a readable reveal rewards rewatching: try it on line 12, a small gold number that grows to
  full size. [startups.co.uk, Nov 2 2026](https://startups.co.uk/news/social-media-trends-november-2026/)
- Late-November topics: Thanksgiving (Nov 26) and Christmas countdowns lead the calendar. A Thanksgiving-week build
  could be the giant parade balloons (helium, ropes and handlers, wind limits; no brand names). Most trends peak
  within 7-10 days. [socialpilot](https://www.socialpilot.co/blog/tiktok-trends)
- Study of 1,200 top science videos (Aiger et al., Computers in Human Behavior): in physics, maths and engineering the
  well-liked videos were objective with little humour; TikTok rewards short, objective, fast delivery with strong
  emotion. Keep the joke in the tag line and lines 1-11 factual and quick. [PsyPost, Mar 30 2026](https://www.psypost.org/how-science-goes-viral-the-hidden-rules-of-tiktok-instagram-and-youtube/)
- Sounds: most trending TikTok sounds are licensed music that business accounts cannot use (filter TikTok Creative
  Center by "Approved for Business Use"). Rising cue: "Minimal for news / news suspense" (~1M est. views/hr, +350%;
  page undated). [vidIQ](https://vidiq.com/trending-tiktok-sounds/), [socialpilot](https://www.socialpilot.co/blog/tiktok-trends)

Free footage and pictures
- After Nov 28 (free Seedance ends): Google Flow gives 50 free credits a day, Veo 3.1 Lite costs 10, so about 5 clips
  of 4-8 s at 720p a day (unused credits lost; free use can be blocked 2-5 PM UTC; watermark and commercial terms
  not stated: check first). PixVerse reportedly 60 credits/day (vendor page, unverified).
  [Hackceleration](https://hackceleration.com/labs/free/ai-video-generator), [Dreamina, Oct 24 2026](https://dreamina.capcut.com/ai-video/free-ai-video-generators-recurring-credits-vs-trials-2026)
- Real archive pictures, free: Library of Congress HAER CA-31 "Golden Gate Bridge" has 41 photos and 6 colour
  transparencies (https://www.loc.gov/item/ca1355); HABS/HAER/HALS covers 43,000+ structures and sites.
  Rights line on the items: "No known restrictions on images made by the U.S. Government; images copied from other
  sources may be restricted" (read each caption page). Good for a real 1930s proof shot or a cover; search it for 84
  Hoover Dam too. [LoC collection](https://www.loc.gov/collections/historic-american-buildings-landscapes-and-engineering-records/about-this-collection/)
- Free stock video, commercial use, no credit needed: Coverr (monetized YouTube allowed, has a Vertical category,
  mixes real and AI clips), Mixkit; Pexels' vertical collection grew in 2025 (release needed for recognisable people
  or property). Videezy free clips need a credit line. [Coverr](https://coverr.co/stock-video-footage/commercial-use),
  [Swarmify, upd. Aug 25 2026](https://swarmify.com/blog/best-free-b-roll-websites/)
- Checked: no current Higgsfield offer makes MCP generation unlimited ("not included on MCP", Higgsfield blog Jul 27
  2026; the "72 h unlimited via MCP" Black Friday deals on mydealz show as expired). The rule stays: website +
  Unlimited switch only. [Higgsfield](https://higgsfield.ai/blog/new-unlimited-more-models),
  [mydealz](https://www.mydealz.de/deals/black-friday-higgsfield-ai-bis-65-auf-jahresabo-3-tage-unbegrenzt-per-mcp-seedance-25-720p-30s-2872735)

## 2026-11-23 22:56 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)
Trends
- "0 to 100% loading" reveal: a loading bar runs 0% to 100%, then the result (Virlo's November list; Buffer pairs it
  with "Windows in Brazil (Sped Up)"). We already draw a thin gold progress bar: show "BUILD 0%" on the hook and let
  it reach 100% on the reveal (line 11). [Virlo, upd. Nov 23 2026](https://virlo.ai/trends/2026-11),
  [Buffer, Nov 3 2026](https://buffer.com/resources/trending-audio-instagram/)
- ON/OFF toggle reveal: a switch flips and the scene changes (Buffer: brands use it "to tease a launch"). Fits our
  break/fix pairs (damper OFF: the tower sways; ON: it stands still). The trend's song is licensed music: use our own
  click + whoosh. [Buffer](https://buffer.com/resources/trending-audio-instagram/)
- YouTube "Shorts series" (episodes and seasons, sequential playback "so you can pick up where you left off") rolled
  out Sep 23 2026 on web, mobile and TV: group the numbered builds into seasons (e.g. the Seedance builds from 76) so
  a viewer binges in order. Testing three cuts of one Short comes "from 2027". [Digital Trends, Sep 23 2026](https://www.digitaltrends.com/home-theater/youtube-is-giving-shorts-a-tv-sized-makeover-complete-with-seasons-episodes-and-sequential-playback/),
  [TechCrunch, Sep 23 2026](https://techcrunch.com/2026/09/23/youtube-adds-new-creator-tools-like-video-a-b-testing-dynamic-thumbnails-and-live-dubbing/)

Free footage and pictures
- Patent drawings are free proof shots for invention builds: USPTO, "the text and drawings of a patent are typically
  not subject to copyright restrictions" (limited exceptions: 37 CFR 1.71(d)(e), 1.84(s); look for a copyright notice
  on the patent). Zipper 81: Sundback's 1917 "Separable Fastener" drawing with a slow zoom for line 12; also the
  pressure cooker, the Thermos. [USPTO terms of use](https://www.uspto.gov/terms-use-uspto-websites),
  [the drawing on commerce.gov](https://2017-2021.commerce.gov/images/diagram-patent-application-g-sundbacks-separable-fastener-known-today-zipper.html)
- After Nov 28: Higgsfield's 11.11 bonus (annual Plus: Seedance 2.5 "14 days, up to 30 sec, 720p") says "When a bonus
  period ends, it does not renew." Its "7-Day Unlimited Bonus" post (Oct 30 2026) lists annual Plus with Seedance 2.5
  "Up to 20 sec, 720p", website only, "available for a limited time", with no end date and no word on how it starts.
  The real end dates are on the site: Manage Account > Subscription > "Active unlimited models". The next browser
  run should read that page and correct HIGGSFIELD.md's table; never activate an offer from a run. [11.11 deals, Nov 11 2026](https://higgsfield.ai/blog/higgsfield-11-11-deals),
  [7-day bonus, Oct 30 2026](https://higgsfield.ai/blog/higgsfield-7-day-unlimited-bonus)
- No free Seedance elsewhere: Dreamina's "four free Seedance 2.5 generations" campaign has "ended" ($0.29 is a paid
  first month, then $15/month). Krea's free tier (100 units a day) lists no commercial licence; Dreamina gives a daily
  free allowance (120 a day in the US) but its page does not state watermark or commercial terms: check before use.
  [Dreamina, Oct 27 2026](https://dreamina.capcut.com/seedance/seedance-2-5-pricing-2026),
  [Dreamina, Nov 6 2026](https://dreamina.capcut.com/ai-video/free-ai-video-tools-compared),
  [Krea](https://www.krea.ai/blog/best-free-ai-video-generators-in-2026)

## 2026-11-23 23:55 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)
Trends
- Best hook type in Virlo's week of Nov 16: "POV" setup hooks, 32.9% of 50,583 TikToks reached its virality score of
  18+, against 22.1% for the most used type, the bold claim (281,715 videos). Our line 1 ("If you ..., congrats, you
  just built ...") is already a second-person setup: keep the "you", never swap it for a bare claim. The week's top
  formats are comedy skits: no fit. [Virlo, upd. Nov 22 2026](https://virlo.ai/trending-on-tiktok-this-week/2026-11-16)

Platform rules
- YouTube AI labels (May 27 2026): if the creator does not answer, YouTube now labels "significant photorealistic AI
  use" itself; on Shorts the label is "an overlay on the video itself". Disclosure is required when a video
  "Generates a realistic scene that didn't actually occur" or alters "footage of a real event or place" (example: "AI
  generated extra footage of a real place"). Repeat non-disclosers "may be subject to manual application of a label,
  or penalties", up to "suspension from the YouTube Partner Program"; the blog says a label itself does not change
  recommendations or monetization. So every Seedance build (76 on) answers Studio's AI question YES. The ready-made
  snippet in YOUTUBE_STUDIO_UPLOAD.md step 5 clicks VIDEO_HAS_ALTERED_CONTENT_NO (the text above it says answer
  truthfully), and nobody has checked whether Buffer's YouTube posts set it at all.
  [YouTube blog, May 27 2026](https://blog.youtube/news-and-events/improving-ai-labels-viewers-creators/?pubDate=20260527),
  [help page](https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/14328491?hl=en),
  [MBW, May 27 2026](https://www.musicbusinessworldwide.com/youtube-will-now-automatically-detect-and-label-ai-videos-even-when-creators-dont-disclose-it/)

Free footage and pictures
- Checked: no Higgsfield offer extends free Seedance past Nov 28. "33 days unlimited Seedance 2.5" was for accounts
  that activated it "before August 9" (annual Plus: 20 s clips); the July Seedance 2.0 Fast promo ended July 17;
  aimojo's "Black Friday" page is from Nov 2025 and covers pictures only. Nov 24-28 stay the last video days.
  [Higgsfield, upd. Oct 1 2026](https://higgsfield.ai/blog/33-days-unlimited-seedance-2-5),
  [Higgsfield, Jul 2 2026](https://geo.higgsfield.ai/blog/higgsfield-seedance-unlimited-plan-changes),
  [aimojo](https://aimojo.io/higgsfield-black-friday-deal/)
- YouTube's own generators are free but stay inside YouTube: Veo 3 in Shorts makes clips "up to eight seconds" with
  sound and needs no Gemini subscription (Sep 2025); Gemini Omni (May 2026) came free to Shorts and YouTube Create,
  every clip "watermarked with SynthID" and labelled AI. No source gives daily limits, download or commercial terms:
  at most a YouTube-only extra, not footage for the cross-posted builds.
  [TechRadar, Sep 18 2025](https://www.techradar.com/ai-platforms-assistants/youtube-shorts-now-lets-you-turn-text-into-8-second-videos-using-veo-3s-ai-magic),
  [9to5Google, May 19 2026](https://9to5google.com/2026/05/19/gemini-omni-create-anything-model-video/)
- Hoover Dam (84) real photos: Reclamation's "Historic Views" gallery has about 15 construction photos (high scalers
  in 1932, the "Jumbo Rig" that drilled the diversion tunnels, the tunnel lining, cofferdams, the powerhouse; no
  block-pour or cooling-pipe picture), and its Hoover Dam collection lists "approximately 20,000 photographs" (search
  PDFs, staff contact on the page). Neither page states a licence: U.S. government work has no copyright
  (17 U.S.C. 105), but check each photo's credit first. Good for a real line 11/12 proof shot.
  [usbr.gov gallery](https://www.usbr.gov/lc/hooverdam/gallery/historicviews.html),
  [collection](https://www.usbr.gov/lc/hooverdam/collection.html)

## 2026-11-24 00:58 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)
Trends
- Show the finished thing first: Lightreel's list for Nov 14-21 names "finished-result or character-first openings"
  (the visual destination in the first seconds, no build-up) and the "tactile demonstration" (one close-up, no pitch,
  "the texture is the hook"). A1 already is the strangest true picture; for everyday builds (81 zipper, 83 Thermos)
  make A1 a macro of the mechanism moving, never a wide room. [Lightreel, upd. Nov 21 2026](https://www.lightreel.ai/blogs/whats-trending-on-tiktok)
- Say the full name out loud: TikTok "can index spoken words", and 49% of consumers use TikTok as a search engine
  (Adobe 2026, quoted by Hootsuite). Keep the build's full searchable name in spoken line 1 or 2 ("the Hong
  Kong-Zhuhai-Macau Bridge", not "this bridge") and first in the caption. [Hootsuite, upd. Oct 14 2026](https://blog.hootsuite.com/tiktok-trends/)
- Nothing new to chase this week: Virlo's Nov 23 list (76 trends) has only comedy, couples and beauty rising, its one
  physical-demo trend (water beading on a jacket) is archived, and SocialPilot has not changed since Nov 17. No sound
  on these pages is marked business-safe. [Virlo, upd. Nov 23 2026](https://virlo.ai/trends/2026-11),
  [SocialPilot](https://www.socialpilot.co/blog/tiktok-trends)

Free footage and pictures
- Smithsonian Open Access: "more than 5.1 million 2D and 3D digital items" to "download, share, and reuse ... without
  asking". Use only items marked CC0 (public domain: commercial use, no credit needed); there is an API and a GitHub
  repository. Technology items include the 1903 Wright Flyer, the Apollo 11 command module, a Bell X-1 3D model and
  patent models: search it for invention builds (zipper, Thermos) before making an AI still.
  [Smithsonian](https://www.si.edu/openaccess),
  [Creative Commons, Feb 27 2020](https://creativecommons.org/2020/02/27/smithsonian-releases-2-8-million-images-data-into-the-public-domain-using-cc0/)
- After Nov 28, one more daily free video source to check: Kling AI's free tier, "66 free credits daily, resetting
  every 24 hours", about 10 credits per standard 5 s clip (about 6 clips a day), "no watermark, up to 720p", per a
  competitor's article; commercial terms for free output not stated: read Kling's own terms first. Same article:
  Runway's free tier is a one-time 125 credits with a watermark and no commercial licence, and Jimeng's free Seedance
  2.0 restricts commercial use outside China: skip both. [dev.to/Genra, upd. Nov 21 2026](https://dev.to/genra_ai/free-ai-video-generator-whats-really-free-in-2026-4a20)

## 2026-11-24 03:01 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)
Platform rules
- TikTok wants a label on every Seedance build: "We also require creators to label all AI-generated content that
  contains realistic images, audio, and video" (examples: "Entirely AI-generated videos or images of real or
  fictional people, places, and events"), and "We may remove content that violates our Community Guidelines
  policies by depicting misleading information or unlabeled AI-generated content". The label "won't affect the
  distribution of your video". Auto labels come only from C2PA Content Credentials, which our ffmpeg cut does not
  carry over, so the label has to come from us. [TikTok support](https://support.tiktok.com/en/using-tiktok/creating-videos/ai-generated-content),
  [TikTok newsroom, Mar 10 2026](https://newsroom.tiktok.com/tiktok-ssa-shares-more-ways-to-spot-shape-and-understand-ai-generated-content?lang=en-ZA)
- Buffer can set it for us: its "AI-Generated" flag covers TikTok ("video posts only", shows "Contains AI-generated
  media"), YouTube Shorts ("ⓘ AI"), Instagram (posts, reels, carousels, stories) and X; the network "applies its own
  native AI-content label", and it can be sent "If you use the Buffer API or the MCP server" (field name not given;
  an unsupported post gets a validation error). TikTok's own API field is post_info.is_aigc ("Set to true if the
  video is AI generated content", optional; shows "Creator labeled as AI-generated"). Today scripts/post.py sends
  TikTok posts with no metadata at all and YouTube/Instagram metadata without any AI flag, so before the first
  Seedance build (78 on: media made, none in posts/ yet) is posted, find the flag's field with scripts/inspect_api.py
  (needs the Buffer key, so in CI) and have post.py set it for `look: sd` builds. This also settles the 23:55 question
  about Buffer's YouTube posts. [Buffer help](https://support.buffer.com/articles/flagging-posts-as-ai-generated-in-buffer-V7jAnzYJ5n),
  [TikTok API, upd. Aug 24 2026](https://developers.tiktok.com/docs/en/content-posting-api-reference-direct-post)

Free footage and pictures
- Hoover Dam (84) real 1930s film: the University of Utah's Marriott Library has "Boulder Dam: The Pictorial Record
  of Man's Conquest of the Colorado River" (1937, creator "United States. Bureau of Reclamation", a 5:22 mp4
  segment, "A segment from a public service announcement produced by the U.S. Bureau of Reclamation"). Its Rights
  field is blank: a federal film has no U.S. copyright (17 U.S.C. 105), but ask the library (or its finding aid)
  before using it as a line 11 proof shot. Skip the Bancroft Library's 1930s Six Companies reels on Calisphere:
  "Copyrighted. Rights are owned by UC Regents". [Utah](https://collections.lib.utah.edu/ark:/87278/s62sehm8),
  [Calisphere](https://calisphere.org/item/ddcbdfe24a3f3088f352f820ee17aedc/)

New content
- A candidate for LINEUP 86 (LINEUP ends at 85, and the Dubai Frame's job B needs build 86 for its B4 tease; an
  everyday thing fits after three big structures): the electric kettle that switches itself off. A "steam channel"
  "carries steam from the top of the boiling interior down to the thermostat", a bimetallic disc that "snaps
  suddenly at a certain temperature" trips the switch (the click), and a plain thermostat in the water would fail
  because the boiling point falls "approximately 0.5°C" per 150 m of height. A1 as a macro of the disc snapping fits
  the tactile-hook note of 00:58. Not made before (episodes.yml has no kettle). [Ingenia](https://www.ingenia.org.uk/articles/how-do-kettles-know-when-to-switch-off),
  [Explain that Stuff](https://www.explainthatstuff.com/how-electric-kettles-work.html)

## 2026-11-24 04:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)
Trends
- Front-load harder on YouTube: Metricool's study of 799,718 videos says Shorts' average view duration "fell about
  67%, down to roughly 16 seconds per Short", and "More than 61% of all organic views in the sample came from the
  Shorts feed". Our builds run 65-70 s against an average view of about 16 s: the finished build on screen in the
  first 2-3 s (A1) and the cut fact by line 2 matter more than anything later. [Metricool, Jul 29 2026, upd. Aug 4](https://metricool.com/youtube-trends/) (vendor)
- Instagram length, the other side: Buffer's Reels-length study says "Reels between 90 seconds and three minutes
  reached 4–7% more people than each account's own average" (seen as a teaser card dated Nov 12 2026; the article
  was not read), against the "15 to 60 seconds" line of 01:58. Test before changing anything: one build cut to
  60 s and one to 90 s, Instagram only. [Buffer](https://buffer.com/resources/trending-audio-instagram/),
  [article](https://buffer.com/resources/instagram-reels-length/)
- A trending sound now lifts photo posts too: it "makes carousels and photos eligible to appear on the reels feed,
  too". A cheap extra post per build while pictures are unlimited (to Jan 13): a carousel of the hero still and two
  cutaway frames with a business-safe sound. [Buffer, Nov 3 2026](https://buffer.com/resources/trending-audio-instagram/) (vendor)

Free footage and pictures
- Prelinger Archives on the Internet Archive: "more than 6600 films" (advertising, educational, industrial and
  amateur films, mostly 1930s-60s). Reuse is "warmly encouraged ... in whole or in part", "Any derivative works
  that you produce using these films are yours", credit appreciated "but do not require it"; read each film's own
  page for its licence ("Those titles that either are copyrighted or whose status is in question have not been
  made available"). Standard definition only (most 480 x 480 or 368 x 480): a short real-archive insert (old
  kitchens and appliances for the everyday builds, factories), not a full-screen hero.
  [Internet Archive help](https://help.archive.org/help/prelinger-archive/),
  [Open Culture, May 3 2016](https://openculture.com/2016/05/download-6600-free-films-from-the-prelinger-archives-and-use-them-however-you-like.html)
- Free-tier re-check (Scrimba, upd. Nov 24 2026): Sora is gone (web and app closed Apr 26 2026, API Sep 24 2026).
  "Runway, Canva and Adobe Express allow commercial use of free output but ration it hard" (Canva: silent 4 s
  clips; Adobe Express: an unpublished number of 5 s clips a day): inserts at most. This contradicts the 00:58 line
  (Runway free = no commercial licence): read Runway's own terms before any use. CapCut calls its generator
  "completely free", but its Materials License works per asset, so one non-commercial template or sound limits the
  whole video. [Scrimba](https://scrimba.com/articles/free-ai-video-generators-2026/) (vendor)

New content
- 85 Dubai Frame queued (dubaiframe-A/B; facts and sources in the queue comments), and LINEUP 86 is now the
  kettle proposed at 03:01. More facts for 86's jobs: the steam tube is "typically inside the handle"; the disc
  flips "a bit like an umbrella turning inside out in the wind" and "you may hear the disk flipping back" as it
  cools; 2400 W boils a litre in "about 160 seconds"; Otter and Strix made "more than a billion thermostats of this
  kind"; before switch-off "many a hotel was evacuated when a left kettle boiled and boiled". Only Morphy Richards (a
  maker's blog, uncorroborated) names the first self-switching kettle: the 1955 Russell Hobbs K1.
  [Ingenia](https://www.ingenia.org.uk/articles/how-do-kettles-know-when-to-switch-off),
  [Explain that Stuff](https://explainthatstuff.com/how-electric-kettles-work.html),
  [Morphy Richards, Dec 22 2025](https://www.morphyrichards.co.uk/blogs/kettle-guides/how-does-a-kettle-work)

  and smoke particles "attract the ions and carry them away, reducing the current"; a photoelectric one sees "light
  reflected off particles from a light beam inside the sensing chamber" (NIST). Ionization alarms "tend to detect
  small black soot particles from flaming fires more quickly", photoelectric ones the larger, light-coloured
  particles of smouldering fires; alarms "near kitchens can detect the particles coming off your food as it cooks";
  a working alarm "cuts your risk of dying in a fire by 55%" (NIST, citing NFPA; Wikipedia says "cut in half"). The
  source is "about 37 kBq ... about 0.3 μg" of americium-241; Walter Jaeger found the effect in the late 1930s when a
  failed poison-gas sensor reacted to his cigarette smoke; Duane Pearsall and Stanley Peterson made "the first
  low-cost smoke detector for domestic use" in 1965 (Wikipedia; its smouldering-fire timings disagree with the NIST
  study it cites: check). The night chirp: a dying battery's internal resistance rises in the cold, and "Most homes
  are the coolest between 2 a.m. and 6 a.m." (Kidde, a maker). B6 writes itself: the 2 a.m. chirp, a broom, a chair.

## 2026-11-24 10:05 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)
Platform rules
- YouTube does not monetize a channel that feels made from one template. Its "Generic or Repetitive Content" rule
  ("inauthentic content" since July 2025) covers "content that looks like it's made with a template, or that may feel
  repetitive to viewers after watching several videos in a row from the same channel", including "AI-generated content
  made with generic or unoriginal templates giving the impression of mass production". It is judged per channel
  ("channels where content feels interchangeable from video to video are not allowed to monetize") and counts Shorts.
  Allowed: the "Same intro and outro for your videos, but the bulk of your content is different", "each video has a
  distinct storyline, focus, or concept", and "using AI to edit your video scripts or generate a unique background
  visual"; the page says nothing about AI voices. TechCrunch: the July 2026 clarification "rolled out on July 16" for
  "all YouTube Partner Program members". Build-It use: six same-shaped Seedance builds a day sit near that line, so keep
  each build's own story and vary the opening format (POV, then-and-now, Day 1 vs later, loading bar: see above), not
  only the topic. [YouTube Help](https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/1311392?hl=en),
  [TechCrunch, Jul 20 2026](https://techcrunch.com/2026/07/20/youtube-clarifies-policies-around-ai-slop-and-upsetting-videos/),
  [Tubefilter, Jul 13 2026](https://www.tubefilter.com/2026/07/13/youtube-inauthentic-content-monetization-policy-update/)

  [Lock 15](https://www.dvidshub.net/video/844608/lock-15-dewatering-time-lapse-b-roll)

New content
- LINEUP 95 is now the Falkirk Wheel (Scotland; Europe's first build since 88), so the smoke alarm pair's B4 has a build
  to tease. It lifts boats 24 m between two canals (ICE, Wikipedia, Hackaday; ASME says 18 m) where a flight of 11 locks
  took "nearly a day" (ICE; ASME "half-day") until they were dismantled in 1933; two more locks climb the last 11 m to
  the Union Canal. Its two water-filled gondolas balance each other: floating boats "displace their own weight in
  water" (Wikipedia), so a gondola weighs the same with or without boats, and ten hydraulic motors (22.5 kW) turn the
  35 m wheel on 1.5 kWh per half-turn, "roughly the same as boiling eight kettles" (Wikipedia; a callback to 86). A fixed
  central gear, two idler gears and three 8 m ring gears keep the gondolas level as the arms turn (Wikipedia; Hackaday:
  "three 8-meter gears"). Built at Butterley in Derbyshire, moved in 35 lorry loads and bolted, not welded, against
  fatigue ("more than 15,000" bolts and "Each bolt was tightened by hand", ICE; "over 14,000", Wikipedia, Hackaday).
  Vandals forced its gates open before the 24 May 2002 opening and delayed it a month (Wikipedia). Check before use:
  water per gondola (250,000 litres in Wikipedia, Hackaday and ASME, 500,000 in ICE), steel (1,200 t ICE, 1,500 t
  ASME), a half-turn (about 4 min Hackaday, 5.5 min ASME). A B6 idea: a man lowers himself into a brimful bath and the
  water floods the floor (Archimedes). [ICE](https://www.ice.org.uk/what-is-civil-engineering/infrastructure-projects/falkirk-wheel),
  [ASME](https://www.asme.org/engineering-topics/articles/transportation/symbol-of-the-millennium-the-falkirk-wheel),
  [Hackaday, Feb 13 2026](https://hackaday.com/2026/02/13/the-engineering-of-the-falkirk-wheel),
  [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falkirk_Wheel)
- Huajiang (93), the cable-wire gap filled: "A single main cable consists of 217 compacted strand bundles, which in turn
  contain 91 high-strength, 5.7 mm steel wires" (China Daily, Apr 26 2025): about 19,700 wires a cable, about 39,500 in
  the two, so People's Daily's "Each cable is formed by nearly 40,000 individual steel wires" (repost, Feb 26 2026)
  fits only as the total; it adds "tensile strength of up to 2,000 megapascals", that the wires' "combined length would
  circle the Earth's equator twice", fiber grating sensors inside the cables tracking "temperature, humidity, and stress
  levels", and a zinc-aluminium-magnesium coating. A new break and fix for its pair: the danger of "an oil tanker
  catching fire on the bridge deck", so the main cables got "up to five layers of fire-resistant material" (under one
  centimetre in all) that "withstand temperatures of up to 1,100 C for a full hour" (China Daily). Ready shots: a
  tanker fire on the deck with flames licking the cable, and a macro cutaway of one cable packed with bundles of wires.
  [China Daily, Apr 26 2025](https://mobile.chinadaily.com.cn/cn/html5/2025-04/26/content_002_680c0be5ed50ccabe15111e5.htm),
  [People's Daily via Alwihda, Feb 26 2026](https://www.alwihdainfo.com/nearly-40000-steel-wires-hold-up-world-s-tallest-bridge-a148378/)

- A candidate for LINEUP 96 (the Falkirk Wheel pair will need a build to tease; 95 is a structure, so an everyday
  thing; it would post in early December, the first cold weeks, and winter desert camping for the Arabic channel): a
  hand warmer. The reusable one is a pouch of clear liquid: "When the internal metal strip is bent, tiny bits of metal
  are released, which offer 'nucleation sites' for crystals to form", "heating the hand warmer up to 54°C", reset by
  "boiling it in a pan of water to liquefy the crystals" (BBC Science Focus); Wikipedia: flexing the disk "generates
  nucleation centers that initiate crystallisation". The disposable one is iron powder rusting in air, with water,
  salt, activated charcoal and vermiculite (HowStuffWorks; Wikipedia lists cellulose instead of water): vermiculite
  "absorbs water", the charcoal "helps disperse the heat evenly" and "controls the pace of the reaction", and it lasts
  "about eight to 10 hours, on average" (HowStuffWorks); it "reaches its maximum temperature in about 20 minutes" and
  "can reach 74 °C (165 °F)" (Wikipedia). Check before use: the first one (HowStuffWorks: Japan "in 1923"; Wikipedia
  ties 1923 to a platinum-catalysed warmer and lists an unbuilt 1891 US patent), and the top temperature (37-82 C in
  HowStuffWorks). Ready shots: the click and a white crystal front racing through the clear pouch, a slow-motion cutaway
  of rust spreading over iron grains, a B6 of someone clicking one by mistake in a pocket on a hot day.
  [BBC Science Focus](https://www.sciencefocus.com/science/how-do-re-usable-hand-warmers-work/),
  [HowStuffWorks](https://science.howstuffworks.com/disposable-hand-warmers.htm),
  [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_warmer)

  roadways" (ASCE). Each panel "is made up of four layers and was created following a complex 16-step process",
  "produced using automated robotic arms", and "only several could be produced per day"; the facade took "around 18
  months" to install (Gulf News); "more than 10,000 bespoke pieces of glass" were cut "using water jets" (ASCE), and
  the calligraphy is "3D-mapped onto the building’s curved body" (Gulf News). Opened 22 Feb 2022 (Buro Happold). Check
  before use: the start year (2015 in ASCE; Buro Happold dates the digital prototype to 2016). Ready shots: thousands
  of trial meshes settling into one steel ring; a robot arm milling one panel mould of 1,024; a truck squeezing a
  diagrid piece through narrow city streets.
  [ASCE, Jan 3 2023](https://asce.org/publications-and-news/civil-engineering-source/civil-engineering-magazine/issues/magazine-issue/article/2023/01/museum-of-the-future-sets-standard-for-whats-to-come-in-digital-design),
  [Gulf News, upd. Feb 16 2022](https://gulfnews.com/uae/revealed-how-dubais-architecturally-unique-museum-of-the-future-was-built-1.85767249),
  [Buro Happold, Feb 21 2022](https://www.burohappold.com/articles/designing-the-diagrid-using-a-bespoke-growth-algorithm-to-deliver-museum-of-the-future/)

- Hand warmer (LINEUP 96), the checks its entry asks for. The first throwaway one: "In 1978, the company became the
  first in Japan to launch iron-filled, portable heating pads it named Hokaron" (Lotte), found by accident in the
  mid-1970s: "By accident, water got mixed into the iron — and, lo and behold, it got warm"; "a total of 1.4 billion
  kairo pads were sold during what they call the 2008 season" (Japan Times, Feb 21 2010, citing the kairo makers'
  association). The 1923 one was a different kind: Hakkin's founder Niichi Matoba "obtained a patent for application of
  the principle of an oxidation reaction that produces heat by means of catalysis of an element of the platinum group"
  (Hakkin history; its fuel is benzine, per its 1998 entry). How the iron pad works: "Fe+3/4O2+3/2H2O → Fe(OH)3"
  (rust), salt "serves as a catalyst and speeds up the heating process", activated carbon "can absorb oxygen",
  vermiculite is "excellent at holding water", and it starts "the moment the outside plastic wrapping is opened and air
  gets in"; the pads "warm up to 50 or 60 C and continue exuding their heat for up to 20 hours" (Japan Times; this
  page's 20 hours against HowStuffWorks' 8-10: say "hours"). The reusable pouch: sodium acetate "supercooled below its
  freezing point of 54°C" stays liquid until crystals get a start ("a scratch, an impurity, a dust speck"); flexing "a
  tiny metal disk" exposes "rough surfaces on the disk" "that allow crystals to grow", and the pad "warms to a toasty
  54°C" (Frostburg State, rev. Feb 23 2018); reset: "Put the packs in very hot or boiling water for a few minutes" (SFU
  Physics demos). A break/fix chain for its pair: rust gives heat, but a rusty nail is never warm (too slow), so powder
  (a huge surface) and salt speed it up; it would burn out in the box, so the wrapper keeps the air out until you tear
  it; and the reusable pouch works by staying liquid far below its 54 C freezing point. Ready shots: water spilled
  on iron powder in a 1970s lab, a wisp of warmth rising; a rusty nail beside a glowing pile of iron dust; the white
  crystal front racing out from the clicked disc. [Japan Times, Feb 21 2010](https://www.japantimes.co.jp/life/2010/02/21/general/feeling-the-heat),
  [Hakkin](https://hakkin.co.jp/history.html),
  [Frostburg State](https://antoine.frostburg.edu/chem/senese/101/consumer/faq/how-do-handwarmers-work.shtml),
  [SFU](https://www.sfu.ca/physics/demos/demos-experiments/heatpackburnaby.html)

New content
- Fire sprinkler, a candidate build (not in LINEUP; it follows the smoke alarm, 94, and the movie myth is its hook):
  "only the sprinkler heads directly in the area of the fire will operate" (FPA, Jun 12 2024); "most fires usually
  require only one or two sprinklers" (HowStuffWorks). In US fires of 2015-2019 where sprinklers operated, one head
  did it in 77% and five or fewer in 97%; the death rate per fire was 89% lower; they operated in 92% of fires big
  enough and were effective in 96% of those, and the main failure was a system "shut off at some point before the
  fire" (NFPA Journal, Spring 2022). How: a glass bulb "filled with a heat sensitive liquid, which is commonly
  glycerin-based" that breaks at "usually 68°C", or "a fusible link made of a low-melting alloy" (FPA); "the liquid
  expands and breaks the glass bulb" (NFSA, Apr 17 2023), coloured by rating: orange and red "from 135 to 170°F
  (57-77°C)", yellow "174°F (79°C)", green "225°F (107°C)", blue "250°F (121°C)" (NFSA, citing NFPA). A head uses
  "about six times less water than a fire hose" (HowStuffWorks). History: Henry Parmelee, "in 1874, to protect his
  piano factory", is "credited with the first automated sprinkler head" (Wikipedia). Check before use: 68 C (FPA)
  against 165 F, 74 C (CDC NIOSH, Oct 11 2022), and when the glass bulb came (not in these pages). Ready shots: A1 macro
  of the liquid swelling until the bulb shatters and water bursts out; A4 the movie version, a whole office drenched;
  A5 the real one, one head over a burning wastebasket; B6 a cake with ninety candles carried under a sprinkler,
  everyone freezes and looks up, nothing happens.
  [FPA, Jun 12 2024](https://www.thefpa.co.uk/advice-and-guidance/advice-and-guidance-articles/how-do-fire-sprinklers-work-),

## 2026-11-24 17:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Sounds and rights
- Our Shorts are over a minute, and YouTube blocks every such Short that carries a claim: "Any Short that is over one
  minute in duration with an active copyright claim of any type, including manual claims, will be blocked globally on
  YouTube"; it is "not playable or recommended" and earns nothing, with "no penalties to your channel", and "Once the
  claim is resolved, your Short will be viewable and eligible for monetization"; under a minute nothing changed
  (YouTube Help, three-minute Shorts). Build-It Shorts run 65-70 s and the show's short cut about 1:20, so one claimed
  sound takes a whole post off YouTube. Our risk is nil today: kit/tools/seedance_cut.py makes the wind bed from noise
  and a 55 Hz drone, and kit/bik/sfx.py makes the thud and the whoosh in code. Build-It use: keep every sound
  generated, and keep "free" library music (Pixabay, the 15:59 entry) out of anything over a minute: "Even if you find
  music online that's labeled "free," Content ID might still flag it", and "Music that's safe to use in a Short that's
  less than 60 seconds may not be safe to use in a longer video" (YouTube Help, safe music tips).
  [YouTube Help, three-minute Shorts](https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/15424877?hl=en),
  [YouTube Help, safe music tips](https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/15577610?hl=en)
- The one claim-proof library is YouTube's own: "Copyright-safe music and sound effects downloaded from the Audio
  Library won't be claimed by a rights holder through the Content ID system"; Partner Program channels can monetize
  with it; Creative Commons tracks need the artist credited in the description (the filter "Attribution not required"
  leaves them out). Off YouTube, Google gives no rule ("YouTube can't give legal guidance, including guidance on issues
  with music that can occur off the platform"); Thematic, a music seller (upd. Oct 5 2026): "Many tracks can be used
  on other platforms, but coverage varies by track and is not guaranteed". Creator Music is YouTube-only: "if you
  license a track from Creator Music, you can only use the track in videos uploaded to YouTube". Build-It use: one file
  goes to YouTube, Instagram and TikTok, so our own sound stays; an Audio Library track or sound effect fits only a
  YouTube-only cut, like the show's full video.
  [YouTube Help, Audio Library](https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/3376882?hl=en),
  [YouTube Help, Creator Music FAQ](https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/11610609?hl=en-AU),
  [Thematic, upd. Oct 5 2026](https://hellothematic.com/youtube-audio-library/)

New content
- Ski Dubai, a candidate Arab-world build (not in LINEUP): real snow inside a desert mall. "Ski Dubai contains 6,000
  tonnes of snow", "The longest run is 400 metres with a fall of over 60 metres" (Alfa Laval, Dec 30 2025); "The ski
  slope's peak is about 85 metres high", while "It may be 50 or so degrees outside" (Gulf News, Nov 1 2018). It is
  "basically a large cold-store lifted up at one end" (Building, undated); ammonia cools "the glycol in the pipe system
  to -16 or -17°C" (Alfa Laval) through "more than 70 km of small-bore polyethylene pipes" in the concrete (Building).
  Snow: "Snow-guns don't actually make snow, they simply atomise water and spray the fine droplets into the air",
  "10-50 microns", "one snow-gun for every 1000 m2" (Building), at "-7 to -8°C" by night (Alfa Laval; Building and
  IIF-IIR say -6). Breaks and fixes on the pages (all Building): humid air gives "a hard layer of blue ice" instead
  of snow (fix: smaller droplets); over-cladding was "essential in preventing the insulation panels bowing under the
  extreme desert heat"; the wall void was closed against "fine talcum-powder like sand"; walls "sealed on the warm
  side of the insulation to prevent condensation". Melted snow is reused for the mall's cooling and irrigation (Alfa
  Laval, Pumps & Systems); "our energy costs are less than 10% of operating costs" (Alfa Laval). Opened "in December
  2005" (Alfa Laval), designed by Acer Snowmec (Building, IIF-IIR). Conflicts, leave out: fresh snow a night ("30–40
  tonnes" Alfa Laval, "30 tons" Pumps & Systems, "between 5 and 25 tonnes, depending on the footfall" Gulf News) and
  the open-hours air (-1 to -2 C in three pages, "always minus eight" in Grundfos's 2005 note). Snowmaking outdoors
  works only "When the wet-bulb temperature is below 28 degrees Fahrenheit or -2 degrees Celsius" (Park City
  Mountain, Nov 22 2022). A chain: 50 C outside → a giant cold box (A3) → its panels bow in the sun (A4) → an outer
  skin (A5) → humid air makes blue ice (A6) → a fine mist from ceiling guns at night (B1) → the day's crowds wear the
  snow down (B2, our reading of "depending on the footfall") → fresh snow every night, the melt reused (B3). Ready
  shots (no faces, no logos, skiers small and from behind): A1 snow falling from ceiling guns over an empty slope at
  night while through the glass end wall the desert city shimmers in heat; a cutaway of glycol pipes in the concrete
  under the snow; B6 funny slow motion, an ice cream carried out of the snow hall melts in three steps across a
  sunny car park. Seedance is free only until Nov 28, so its pair would have to run this week (the owner's or the
  main chat's call; LINEUP 98-105 have no pairs yet either).
  [Alfa Laval, Dec 30 2025](https://www.alfalaval.com/media/stories/cooling/skiing-in-the-desert/),
  [Building](https://building.co.uk/sun-sea-and-snow/3054370.article),
  [Gulf News, Nov 1 2018](https://gulfnews.com/uae/environment/why-ski-dubai-is-the-coolest-place-in-town-these-days-1.2051177),
  [IIF-IIR, Oct 26 2005](https://iifiir.org/en/news/out-of-ordinary-skiing-under-a-hot-desert-sun),
  [Pumps & Systems](https://pumpsandsystems.com/pumps-outsmart-nature-create-fresh-snow-desert),
  [Grundfos, Oct 21 2005](https://www.grundfos.com/media/latest-news/the-coolest-thing-todoindubai),
  [Park City Mountain, Nov 22 2022](https://www.parkcitymountain.com/blog/science-of-snowmaking.aspx)
- String lights (fairy lights), a candidate everyday build for the winter-lights season (not in LINEUP; shown on
  balconies, streets and palm trees, not as a religious holiday): "A mini-light is a small, 2.5-volt incandescent
  bulb" and "If you multiply 2.5 volts by 48, you get 120 volts", so the bulbs sit in series and "When a filament in
  one bulb blows out, it creates an open circuit in the wiring"; without a shunt "any bulb burning out would darken
  the entire strand". The fix is "a shunt wire wrapped around the two posts inside the bulb", whose coating "gives it
  fairly high resistance until the filament fails", then "heat caused by current flowing through the shunt burns off
  the coating" (HowStuffWorks, undated; DOE: "the high temperature from the burnout will cause the substance coating
  the shunt to melt off"). But "Unless there is a shunt in the socket, a loose bulb will cause the whole 50-bulb strand
  to fail", which is why long strands are two or three 50-bulb strands in parallel (HowStuffWorks). LEDs: "An LED bulb
  may last 50,000-200,000 hours while the incandescent bulb will burn for 3,000 hours" (HowStuffWorks); a 6-foot tree
  lit 12 hours a day for 40 days costs "$0.27" against "$10" (DOE, Dec 16 2015: line 12). Energy saved "70 percent"
  (DOE) or "80 percent" (HowStuffWorks): leave the percentage out. The 120 V sum is the US one: check a 230 V
  string before scripting for the Gulf. Chain: tiny cheap bulbs → in series (A3) → one dead filament darkens all (A4)
  → the shunt (A5) → a loose bulb still does (A6) → strands in parallel (B1) → the power bill (B2) → LEDs (B3). Ready
  shots: A1 macro inside a glowing mini bulb as its filament snaps and a whole lit balcony goes dark; a macro of the
  shunt's coating burning away and the light coming back; B6 funny slow motion, a cat bats one bulb loose and the
  balcony goes dark. Same Nov 28 point as Ski Dubai.
  [HowStuffWorks, How Christmas Lights Work](https://people.howstuffworks.com/culture-traditions/holidays-christmas/christmas-lights1.htm),
  [DOE, Dec 16 2015](https://www.energy.gov/node/997476)

- YouTube counts a Shorts view on every start and replay (new to these notes): since Mar 31 2025 "Shorts views will
  now count the number of times a creator's Short starts to play or replay" (TechCrunch, Mar 26 2025), "with no
  minimum watch time requirement" (Search Engine Journal, Mar 26 2025). The old count, "now called 'engaged views'",
  is "available in YouTube Analytics' Advanced Mode only", and YPP eligibility and earnings stay "based on 'engaged
  views'" (SEJ; TechCrunch: the change "won't impact creators' earnings"). Instagram counts Reels the same way ("the
  number of times your reel starts to play or replay", its help text quoted by Tubefilter, Mar 26 2025); TikTok has
  not said how it counts (Tubefilter). Build-It use: a YouTube "view" includes every swipe-past, so the numbers study
  (claude/build-it-performance.md) should compare YouTube by engaged views and average time watched, and read YPP
  progress on engaged views. None of the pages says loops or replays are rewarded; no later change found.

  up, so the top turbine would race (B2) → the towers narrow as they rise (B3). Ready shots (the building only, no
  flags or writing): A1 at sunrise three giant turbines spinning on bridges between two sail-shaped towers over the
  Gulf; B6 funny slow motion, a man seen from behind walks between two tall buildings and the wind turns his umbrella
  inside out.

Platform rules
- Instagram trial reels can be posted through Buffer (new to these notes): Meta's developer blog says "We are
  enhancing the Instagram media Content Publishing API to support the creation of Trial Reels" (Dec 3 2025), and
  Buffer's help page (undated) lists Trial Reel for Reels with three settings, Off, Auto and Manual. "A trial reel is
  shown to people who don't follow you first"; on Auto, "If it performs well, Instagram shows it to your followers
  after 72 hours"; on Manual, "You share it with your followers when you're ready" (Buffer). Limits: a professional
  account; Instagram can refuse a trial that "does not meet its follower requirement" (no number given); Buffer
  "can't show whether your trial reel moved on to your followers". Planoly (upd. Oct 23 2026) names the API setting
  trial_params (MANUAL or SS_PERFORMANCE); Publer's Feb 5 2026 line that Buffer only sends reminders is older than
  Buffer's page. Build-It use: a hook A/B test on Instagram now (YouTube's three-cut test comes "from 2027", Nov 23
  22:56 entry): post a build's second cut with another hook as a trial reel on Auto and compare it with the main
  post. scripts/post.py sends Instagram `{type: reel, shouldShareToFeed: true}`; whether Buffer's API takes a trial
  setting is unchecked (scripts/inspect_api.py, run in Actions, prints the input types). The production chat decides.
  [Meta for Developers, Dec 3 2025](https://developers.facebook.com/blog/post/2025/12/03/instragram-api-updates/),
  [Buffer help](https://support.buffer.com/articles/scheduling-instagram-posts-reels-stories-and-notifications-3XA98S9Q5p),
  [Planoly, upd. Oct 23 2026](https://www.planoly.com/blog/can-you-schedule-trial-reels),
  [Publer, Feb 5 2026](https://publer.com/blog/en/best-tools-to-schedule-instagram-trial-reels/)

  Attribution-Share Alike": 43913 (the dike at 25, 1957) and the Delta Works films "De Pluimpot is dicht" (15 Oct
  1957) and "Zes jaar na de stormvloed" (21 Jan 1959): share-alike could bind our whole video, so leave those out.
  Build-It use: Public Domain Mark items only, one archive proof shot (B5) inside our own footage, shots without

New content
- The Afsluitdijk (Netherlands), a candidate build (not in LINEUP; Build 41 was the Maeslantkering) whose B5 can be
  the real closing of May 1932 from the Public Domain Mark newsreel above. "32 km long and 90 meters wide" (Wikipedia,
  Zuiderzee Works; Wikiwand and the IET blog the same), built 1927-1932; the last gap, the Vlieter, closed on 28 May
  1932 (Wikiwand, IET, Rijkswaterstaat; Wikipedia's table says 23 May: say "May 1932"); "after the flood of 1916 and
  the famine of 1918, opinions were ready for this mega project" (Wikiwand). Build: a sand core, "the seaward side
  is boulder clay with brushwood mattresses above, weighed down by basalt boulders and old concrete", and "boulder
  clay was superior to just sand or clay" (Wikiwand). The hard part: as the gaps narrowed the tidal currents got
  "much stronger" (Wikiwand, Wikipedia); Lorentz "calculated the force of the tide as the smaller gap made it
  stronger"; work ran from four points, two of them building islands (Kornwerderzand, Breezanddijk); the Vlieter
  was closed "by a final bucket of till" (Wikiwand; the 1932 newsreel shows the bucket). Water out: 25 sluices,
  "three series of five" at Den Oever beside the Stevin lock and "two series of five" at Kornwerderzand beside the
  Lorentz locks (Wikiwand, Wikipedia), letting water out only when the Wadden Sea is lower than the lake ("Ik kan
  eigenlijk alleen spuien als het water in Waddenzee lager is dan in het IJsselmeer", Rijkswaterstaat). Then "The
  IJsselmeer was born, even though it was still salty at the time" (Wikiwand). Rijkswaterstaat's page (modified Jul 9
  2026) has work on the dike "Van 2019 tot 2023" and "een Vismigratierivier" at Kornwerderzand (check whether it is
  finished). Leave out or check: the 1916 death toll (IET 53, a Rijkswaterstaat podcast 19), Rijkswaterstaat's 1920
  start, the opening date, the height, the pump figure and the new block names. Chain (A4 and B2 are our reading):
  an inland sea floods the land around it (A2) → one long dike across its mouth (A3) → every gap closed makes the
  tide race faster through the others (A4) → boulder clay the current cannot wash away (A5) → the last gap, the
  Vlieter, is the fiercest (A6) → closed with a final bucket of clay (B1) → the rivers keep pouring in and the lake
  would rise (B2) → 25 sluices that open at low tide (B3). Ready shots (no people): A1 aerial at sunrise, a
  dead-straight dike and road running across open sea to the horizon, waves on one side, a calm lake on the other;
  B6 funny slow motion, a dog digs through a sandcastle wall on the beach and the sea floods the moat. Like Ski Dubai
  (20:00 entry), a Seedance pair would have to run by Nov 28; after that, stills and the newsreel.
  [Wikiwand, Afsluitdijk](https://www.wikiwand.com/en/articles/Afsluitdijk),
  [Wikipedia, Zuiderzee Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuiderzeeworks),
  [IET EngX](https://engx.theiet.org/b/blogs/posts/amsterdammed),
  [Rijkswaterstaat](https://www.rijkswaterstaat.nl/water/waterbeheer/bescherming-tegen-het-water/waterkeringen/dijken/afsluitdijk)

Platform rules
- Custom Shorts thumbnails are open to every creator (new to these notes): YouTube "finally allowed all creators to
  assign customized thumbnails to their Shorts" (Tubefilter, Nov 25 2026); "All users can now add custom thumbnails"
  (Social Media Today, Nov 24 2026). Neal Mohan, quoted by Tubefilter: "Tap the pencil icon on any Short in @YouTube
  Studio to upload yours", on "desktop and mobile"; SMT (citing Rene Ritchie) says it can be set at upload and changed
  "after publishing". Until now only YouTube Partner Program channels had it (since June per SMT, July per Tubefilter

  three pages gives a size or aspect ratio, says where the picture shows (the swiping feed, the channel's Shorts tab,

  saying in 2024 that Shorts thumbnails "were not as important"). Build-It use: both channels are verified

  1080x1920 BUILD #N frame) on its Short in Studio, with the thumbnail call it already makes for the show's full
  videos or with the pencil icon in Content afterwards. Buffer still cannot (kit/series/NIGHT_NOTES.md:
  thumbnailOffset stays null on YouTube, thumbnailUrl untested). Try one Short first and see what size Studio takes;
  the main chat decides.

New content
- The Danjiang Bridge (New Taipei), a candidate build (not in LINEUP; new to these notes) and a sequel to Taipei 101
  (83): "the world's longest single-tower, asymmetric cable-stayed bridge" (Zaha Hadid Architects, Oct 22 2025;
  Parametric Architecture, May 28 2026, and TVBS say the same), across the mouth of the Tamsui River, "officially
  opening to traffic on May 12, 2026" (Parametric; Wikipedia's infobox the same). One "200-metre concrete mast" holds
  a "450-metre central span" (ZHA; Wikipedia and Parametric agree), about 920 m long in all (Wikipedia, Parametric).
  Why: to take traffic off "the existing Guandu Bridge 5km upriver" (ZHA), "nearly 15 kilometers" less driving and
  about 25 minutes off a commute (Parametric). The mast's place came from "detailed 3D modelling and mapping" of the
  estuary, for the ships and "without impairing views of the setting sun" (ZHA); the deck carries cars, buses,
  scooters, bicycles and people on foot, with a light rail line planned (Parametric, ZHA). It won its competition in
  2015, "one of Hadid's final major works before her death in 2016" (TVBS). The engineering is thin so far: the
  pages name only "frequent earthquakes, strong coastal winds, typhoon exposure", "Advanced anti-earthquake technology
  and carefully engineered steel deck systems" (Parametric) and crews who "adjust cable forces" (ZHA); nothing on the
  foundations, the back span, dampers or wind tunnels, so a production run needs a deeper source before scripting.
  Leave out the deck width (71 m in Wikipedia only), the start of work (October 2014 in Wikipedia and TVBS, 2019 in
  Parametric), the cost (no page gives one) and the opening speeches. Ready shots (no people, flags or writing): A1 at
  sunset one slender white mast rising from a river mouth, cables fanning down to a long deck, the sun sinking into
  the sea behind it, slow drone orbit; B6 funny slow motion, a waiter seen from behind balances a long tray of
  teacups on one raised hand, it tips and the cups slide off one by one. Like the Afsluitdijk (23:00 entry), a
  Seedance pair would have to run by Nov 28; after that, stills.
  [ZHA, Oct 22 2025](https://www.zha.com/media-hub/news/2025-10-22-danjiang-bridge-to-open-12-may-2026),
  [Parametric Architecture, May 28 2026](https://parametric-architecture.com/taiwan-opens-danjiang-bridge/),

New content
- Checked facts and a shot chain for LINEUP 105, the Brooklyn Bridge (new to these notes). B5 is the real proof: "On
  the evening of May 17, 1884, just shy of the anniversary of the panic, Barnum led a procession of 21 elephants and
  17 camels across the bridge, with Jumbo bringing up the rear"; the directors "felt they could convince the public
  the bridge was safe if the largest animal in the world walked across it" (History.com, upd. Aug 17 2026). The
  towers stand on caissons, "large (very large) boxes that were placed open-side-down on the river bottom and then
  pressurized" (Linda Hall, May 26 2023); "compressed air was pumped into the chamber, expelling the water"
  (STRUCTURE, Nov 2016). Brooklyn's stopped at "44½ feet"; Manhattan's "at a depth of 78 feet 6 inches, Roebling
  decided the dense sand and gravel would support his tower", with rock at "106 feet" (STRUCTURE; AISC: "stopped the
  work before completely reaching bedrock"). The men got "caisson disease, the Bends" (STRUCTURE), and Washington
  Roebling, "struck down with the bends", "worked entirely from home" (Linda Hall), Emily Roebling serving "as a
  communication liaison with the engineers on site" (AISC, June 2011). The wire: "the Haigh firm was sometimes
  delivering rejected wire rather than the good wire passed by inspectors", so "Roebling required 150 more good steel
  wires per cable than originally planned" (AISC; STRUCTURE: free of charge). Against wind (suspension bridges "known
  to collapse under their own weight, heavy traffic or strong winds", History.com) the elder Roebling "believed that a
  heavy, stiff deck was the key", "an 85-ft wide stiff steel truss", plus "400 diagonal stays" (AISC). Span "1,595.5
  ft", towers "276.5 ft above mean high water" (AISC). Leave out or check: the wires per cable (5,434 in AISC, 5,282
  planned in STRUCTURE), the safety factor (six planned in AISC, five after the fraud in STRUCTURE and Linda Hall),
  the opening day (May 24 in AISC and Linda Hall, May 23 in STRUCTURE: say "May 1883"), the caisson deaths (three in
  AISC, about five in STRUCTURE) and the May 30 1883 crush. Chain (A2 and B2 are our reading): a river too wide and
  busy for anything but one huge span (A2) → granite towers on pressurized timber boxes sunk into the riverbed (A3) →
  the men coming up from the compressed air fall sick with the bends (A4) → stop on firm sand and gravel short of the
  rock, the orders carried from a sickroom (A5) → a crooked contractor swaps in rejected wire overnight (A6) → 150
  extra good wires in every cable (B1) → the wind that tore earlier suspension bridges apart (B2) → a stiff steel truss
  deck and diagonal stays (B3) → the elephants (B5). B4 teases whatever is added as 106. Ready shots (the Roeblings,
  Haigh and Barnum never shown, people only from behind): A1 cutaway of a giant timber box sunk upside down on a
  riverbed, the water held out by compressed air, men seen from behind digging by lamplight inside it, a stone
  tower standing on its roof; B5 at dusk a line of elephants walks across a great stone-towered suspension bridge under
  gas lamps, crowds seen from behind watching, slow drone push; B6 funny slow motion, a man seen from behind sends his
  dog across a wobbly plank over a muddy puddle first, the plank holds the dog and he still falls in.
  [History.com, upd. Aug 17 2026](https://www.history.com/articles/brooklyn-bridge-elephant-procession-p-t-barnum-jumbo-1884),
  [AISC Modern Steel, June 2011](https://www.aisc.org/globalassets/modern-steel/archives/2011/06/2011v06_brooklyn_bridge.pdf),
  [STRUCTURE, Nov 2016](https://www.structuremag.org/article/brooklyn-bridge-part-2/),
  [Linda Hall, May 26 2023](https://www.lindahall.org/about/news/scientist-of-the-day/washington-a-roebling/)

  advertising or promotion" (Terms of Use for CERN Audiovisual Media, read Nov 25 2026); the press room's downloads
  (4K accelerator footage, LHC magnet animations) "can be used on the condition that it is properly credited to CERN
  and that you agree to our terms of use", and CERN's one Creative Commons photo collection is "CC-BY-SA"
  (share-alike, which these notes keep out). So the LHC below would be Seedance footage and our own unlimited stills.
  [CERN terms of use](https://copyright.web.cern.ch/), [CERN press resources](https://home.cern/press/resources/),
  [CERN, CC photos](https://home.cern/news/news/cern/cern-releases-photos-under-creative-commons-licence)

New content
- The Large Hadron Collider near Geneva, a candidate build (new to these notes; not in LINEUP; Europe): "approximately
  27km in circumference" in "a tunnel 100 metres underground", "1232 dipole magnets, 15 metres in length, which bend
  the beams", chilling the magnets "to -271.3°C – a temperature colder than outer space" (1.9 K); "It first started up
  on 10 September 2008" (CERN, facts and figures). "Just nine days after it was first turned on" (Physics Today, Oct 1
  2009), "a faulty electrical connection between two of the accelerator's magnets" let "an electrical arc" develop,
  "puncturing the helium enclosure" (CERN, Oct 16 2008), with "the loss of some six tonnes of liquid helium" (Physics
  World, Oct 17 2008). The repair: "53 magnets had to be removed" ("40 million Swiss francs"), the team "tested some
  20 000 splices" and "added pressure relief valves", and in a quench "the energy will be removed quickly" (Physics
  Today); first collisions 23 November 2009 (CERN). Check before use: "at most 5 quadrupoles and 24 dipoles" (CERN,
  Oct 2008) counts the damaged magnets, 53 the ones taken out; B5's date (the July 2012 Higgs announcement) is not on
  the pages read here; and CERN's page says the LHC runs "until the Long Shut Down 3, starting in July 2026", so it
  may be stopped now (no "today" line). Chain (A2, A4, B2 and B3 are our reading): to see inside a proton you smash
  protons head-on at nearly the speed of light, and a straight track would run off across the countryside (A2) →
  bend the beams round a 27 km ring with 1,232 magnets (A3) → magnets that strong in plain copper would overheat (A4)
  → superconducting magnets bathed in liquid helium colder than outer space (A5) → nine days in, one bad joint between
  two magnets arcs and six tonnes of helium burst out (A6) → 53 magnets out, 20,000 joints tested, relief valves and
  faster quench protection (B1) → B2 and B3 still open (the beams' own energy and the dump that swallows them: not on
  the pages read) → B4 teases whatever follows it in LINEUP → B5 the discovery announcement, an auditorium of
  scientists seen from behind on their feet applauding. Ready shots (no faces, no logos, no writing): A1 at dawn the
  camera dives from green fields and villages below snowy mountains down a deep round shaft into a curved blue tunnel
  lined with giant steel cylinders that runs out of sight both ways, slow push-in; A6 inside the tunnel a blinding
  electric arc flashes between two giant magnets and white clouds of helium blast out and fill the tunnel, warning
  lights spinning, wide view; B6 funny slow motion, a fridge magnet slowly slides down a fridge door under a thick
  stack of papers and drops them across the kitchen floor, a cat watching.
  [CERN, LHC facts and figures](https://home.cern/resources/faqs/facts-and-figures-about-lhc),
  [CERN, Oct 16 2008](https://home.cern/cern-releases-analysis-of-lhc-incident),
  [Physics World, Oct 17 2008](https://physicsworld.com/a/lhc-report-confirms-electrical-fault/),
  [Physics Today, Oct 1 2009](https://physicstoday.aip.org/news/mostly-recovered-the-lhc-readies-for-restart)

  living-room pair (stills every 30 s, no video; page upd. Sep 9 2025) both smoke alarms went off within 40 s, the
  sprinkler in Room B opened at 85 s, and Room A, without one, flashed over at 195 s at over 600 °C (1100 °F). Its
  dormitory sleeping-room tests are five silent videos, among them Room 2 (door closed, sprinkler at 105 s, flames out
  by 125 s, 5:25 long) and Room 4 (door open, no sprinkler, burning until a fire hose at 465 s, 10:26 long); the
  files' host did not show in the page text, so find them in Chrome (or on NIST's YouTube) before counting on them.
  NIST's 2007 testimony gives the trigger as a "temperature sensitive link", "typically 165 ºF" (74 °C), the CDC NIOSH
  figure of the Nov 24 15:05 entry, so "about 70 degrees" fits both it and the 68 C red bulb. Build-It use: line 12's
  stat "85 seconds" against "195", and B5 as the real Room 2 beside Room 4 instead of a Seedance shot.
  [NIST copyright](https://www.nist.gov/copyrights-disclaimers),
  [NIST living room](https://www.nist.gov/property-fieldsection/video-residential-fire-sprinklers),
  [NIST dormitories](https://www.nist.gov/el/fire-research-division-73300/firegov-fire-service/video-impact-sprinklers-fire-hazard),
  [NIST testimony, Jun 20 2007](https://www.nist.gov/speech-testimony/sprinkler-research)

New content
- The barcode, a candidate build for the shopping season (new to these notes; not in LINEUP): Black Friday is Fri
  Nov 27, every checkout beeps, and the stripes are about to share the pack. GS1 wants QR codes with GS1 standards
  "widely adopted" "by the end of 2027", with retailers "around the world having the ability to scan these barcodes
  at Point of Sale" (GS1 statement, 2024; the push is called Sunrise 2027), and brands keep "both the 1D and 2D
  barcode on for a time period"; "Some 2,000 retailers and 1,900 brands have been involved in national pilots or
  implementations to 2D" (Packaging Dive, Oct 27 2026). A chain from checked facts (the breaks are ours): A1, lines
  drawn in beach sand become a barcode under a red beam: in "January 1949", "sitting on Miami Beach", Woodland "poked
  my four fingers into the sand" and pulled "four lines": "wide lines and narrow lines, instead of dots and dashes"
  (Smithsonian Magazine). A2, cashiers "manually entering the price of each on a cash register" (Smithsonian Magazine,
  scanner article). A3, the lines swept into a bull's-eye that "could be read accurately from any angle" (patent
  "filed" in 1949, "granted in 1952"). A4, nothing could read it yet: "a powerful 500-watt incandescent bulb" in the
  home prototype, and no "minicomputer" or "very bright light" (Smithsonian). A5, the laser: "a helium-neon laser
  projected a beam onto a rotating mirror and thence up through a glass plate", read by "a photo-diode" (the
  Smithsonian's Spectra-Physics Model A of 1974). A6, the bull's-eye was "prone to smearing when printed" (IBM); its
  store test ran at the Kroger Kenwood Plaza store in Cincinnati from "July 3, 1972" (Smithsonian). B1, Laurer's
  rectangular code, the winner on March 30, 1973: "30 black and 29 blank vertical lines that wouldn't smudge" (IBM).
  B2, a pack slid across sideways that will not beep (ours); B3, the beam off the rotating mirror reads the code
  "omnidirectionally" (IBM). B5, "just after 8 a.m. on June 26, 1974" at Troy's Marsh Supermarket in Ohio, "a 10-pack
  of Wrigley's Juicy Fruit gum that cost 67 cents" (GS1; the Smithsonian says "Legend has it"), drawn as a plain pack
  with no brand. Line 12: "some 6 billion swipes of a barcode happen every day around the globe" (IBM, Nov 10 2025):
  "6 BILLION". Line 13, the twist: the square code can also carry the "expiry date, lot number, serial number" (GS1).
  B6: a cat hops onto the self-checkout's bagging scale, funny slow motion. Not free: the Smithsonian's photo of the
  scanner ("There are restrictions for re-using this image"; only its text is CC0), and the gum pack "never came to
  the Smithsonian" (it stayed with Marsh's Clyde Dawson), so B5 stays Seedance. No faces (sand, hands, a cashier from
  behind), no store or brand names on screen.

New content
- Harbin's Ice and Snow World, a candidate build for December (new to these notes; not in LINEUP): a city of ice palaces
  built every winter from blocks cut out of the frozen Songhua River, gone every spring. This winter's is being made now:
  "The artificial snow-making work of the 28th Harbin Ice-Snow World officially started" on Wed Nov 18 (Xinhua, Nov 19
  2026); the park grows to "1.25 million square meters" with "400,000 cubic meters of ice and snow" (Xinhua, Oct 13
  2026) and is "likely to open around December 20, 2026" (China Highlights, an estimate). Last winter's opened Wed Dec 17
  2025 and "closed on Saturday as rising temperature led to melting" (Feb 21 2026). A chain from checked facts (breaks
  marked "ours" are ours): A1, the whole city of ice palaces glowing at night. A2, the frozen river at about "minus 20
  degrees Celsius" by day (Malay Mail, Dec 23 2024): its only building stone is water. A3, the river cut into bricks:
  ice "at least 30 centimeters" thick, shaped "using mallets and chisels" and then "chainsaws" into blocks "1.6 meters
  long and 0.8 meters wide" (People's Daily, Dec 9 2024) of about "400-kilo" (Euronews 2019), dragged out "hooking it
  with iron poles" (China Daily, Dec 7 2025), "about 10,000 cubic meters" a day from "nearly 1,000 workers" (CGTN, Dec 10
  2025). A4, machine-made ice "often contains air bubbles and lacks the strength needed for carving" (China Daily, Nov 27
  2025; "isn't strong enough to stand in the wind", Malay Mail, Dec 23 2020): a cloudy block cracks under the chisel. A5,
  river ice is "prized for their uniform density and clear texture" (China Daily, Dec 7 2025) and laid up like bricks:
  last winter's main tower alone "costs 39,000 cubic meters of ice" (Xinhua, Dec 11 2025). A6, a warm autumn: ice
  normally "57 or 58 centimetres" was "about 44 or 45" and the freeze came late (Malay Mail, Dec 23 2024), yet the park
  opens in mid-December. B1, keep last winter's ice: "200,000 cubic meters" stored "for more than 10 months" by "low
  temperature, light avoidance, insulation and sealing", unmelted "even when temperatures exceeded 30 C" (China Daily,
  Nov 27 2025), so "About 10,000 workers" start in late November (Xinhua, Nov 25 2025). B2 (ours), a tall thin tower of
  plain ice snaps in the wind; B3, ice with "Natural fibers, such as wood" blended in, sprayed "25 cm" thick onto an
  inflated balloon that is then removed, made the 31 m Flamenco Ice Tower in Harbin in 2018 (ArchDaily, Jan 15 2018;
  TU/e with the Harbin Institute of Technology; not the park's own method, say so). B5, opening night, crowds from
  behind, the 521 m ice slide (People's Daily, Dec 18 2025) and "more than 120,000 visitors in a single day" (Xinhua, Oct
  13 2026). Line 12: "400,000 CUBIC METERS". Line 13: the city melts every spring, but part of each winter's river ice
  is kept to build the next one (B1). B6: hot water thrown into the freezing air bursts into a cloud of snow, the man
  seen from behind, funny slow motion. Check before a line uses it: how the blocks are bonded (not in any page read) and
  how they are lit (Smithsonian's 2019 piece says "multi-colored incandescent lights"; newer pages say only "lights").
  Free video ends Nov 28, so if the main chat wants this build, its pair has to run by then; no faces (workers from
  behind, gloves, crowds far off).

New content
- The ice resurfacer, a candidate everyday build for rink season (new to these notes; not in LINEUP, and no rink
  build among the made ones): outdoor rinks opened this month (Reston Town Center Nov 6, Colorado Springs' Skate in the
  Park Nov 11, Chicago's list of Nov 13: search results, pages not read), and the machine that irons the ice between
  periods is already a STEM topic for the NHL (the Boston Bruins' "STEM week" video on it: search result, not watched).
  Checked facts: the first one was "developed by American inventor and engineer Frank Zamboni in 1949 in Paramount,
  California" (Wikipedia); his rink opened "in 1940" (TIME, HowStuffWorks; Wikipedia says 1939: check). Before it,
  "three or four workers had to scrape, wash, and squeegee the ice" (Wikipedia), using "a tractor with a blade"
  (HowStuffWorks); "The process took about 90 minutes" and "Skaters either moved through sludge on bad ice, or waited"
  (TIME). The Model A was "Built on a Jeep platform" (TIME; an "army surplus" chassis, Wikipedia) and gave the rink "a
  clean sheet in 15 minutes" (TIME); "patented in 1953" (Wikipedia, TIME). How it works (HowStuffWorks unless named): a
  blade "ranging from 77-96 inches" ("an 80-inch-wide stainless-steel blade", Penn Today) shaves the top, "NHL teams
  like a 1/32 inch trim"; "A horizontal auger conveyor collects these ice shavings, or snow, and funnels them to a
  vertical auger" that throws them into the snow tank (Wikipedia), "about 60 cubic feet" each time; "nozzles forcefully
  spray water into the ice surface" and a rubber squeegee lets "a vacuum nozzle" pick up the dirty water (Wikipedia;
  HowStuffWorks puts the squeegee in front); last, "warm water (140 to 145 degrees F or 60 to 63 degrees C)" runs out
  the back "where a towel smoothes it". It moves at "about 9 miles (14.5 kilometers) per hour", weighs "7,000 to 11,000
  pounds" loaded, and "you basically drive in an ellipse" (Penn Today). Why hot water: it "slightly melts the layer of
  ice below it" (Wikipedia; "it melts that top layer", a maintenance manager in Exploratorium), and some operators
  "believe it forms a stronger bond" (Wikipedia). Leave out "hot water freezes faster" (Wikipedia: "the unproven Mpemba
  effect") and "fewer bubbles" (only an ice-cube answer, "at least in theory", Illinois Physics Van). The sheet: "only
  one inch thick" (Exploratorium; "1-1/4 inch", CBS Minnesota), made white with "a white powder" mixed into the water,
  over "10 miles of piping" of glycol at an NHL arena (CBS Minnesota); "When ice builds up along the boards, it starts
  to create a bowl" (Penn Today). A chain from those facts (breaks marked "ours" are ours): A1, top-down drone shot of
  the machine gliding in an ellipse over a scarred white rink, a strip of mirror-glass ice behind it. A2, a 1940s rink,
  a crew with a tractor blade, hoses and squeegees, skaters waiting at the boards, all from behind: 90 minutes. A3,
  macro of the long blade peeling a 1/32-inch curl of ice. A4 (ours), the shavings pile into a snowdrift in front of
  the blade. A5, cutaway of the horizontal auger feeding the vertical one, snow spraying up into the tank. A6 (ours),
  grit and skate dust left in the grooves. B1, wash nozzles blast the grooves, a squeegee and a vacuum slurp the grey
  water back up. B2 (ours), cold water laid on the cold sheet freezes as a loose skin that skates chip (say it only as
  the operators' belief). B3, 60 C water through a dragged towel melts the top skin and the film freezes into glass.
  B4, a tease of whichever build follows it. B5, a packed arena between periods, the machine's ellipse, then skaters
  gliding onto the mirror, the crowd far off (the 1949 rink seems to skate on as LA Kings Iceland in Paramount: NHL.com
  search result, check). Line 12: "15 MINUTES" (from 90). Line 13, the twist: all that polish rides on a sheet about
  one inch thick over miles of frozen pipe. B6: funny slow motion, a cat riding a robot mop in neat ellipses across a
  wet kitchen floor. Keep the maker's name off screen (it is a trademark and real machines wear it), the driver only
  from behind. Free video ends Nov 28, so if the main chat wants this build, its pair has to run by then.

  with Photon and Ray3.14 video draft resolution" (upd. Jan 25 2026). Pika's Free has monthly credits "0 · credit packs
  only" and lists "Not included: Commercial license" (pricing page, "Last updated: October 28, 2026"; Starter is the
  first plan with one). Luma's own Pika comparison (Sep 15 2026) still claims free credits and commercial use: outdated.
  [Luma licensing](https://lumalabs.ai/learning-hub/licensing),
  [Luma subscriptions](https://lumalabs.ai/learning-hub/payments-subscriptions),
  [Pika pricing](https://pika.art/pricing)

New content
- Road salt, "the road that won't freeze", a candidate winter build (new to these notes; not in LINEUP, and no de-icing
  build among the made ones). Checked facts: "As salt is added, the freezing point drops" and "As salt dissolves in
  water, it breaks down into two ions" (PBS NewsHour, Jan 18 2011). Crews' brine is "a 23% concentration of rock salt to
  water" (Peel Region); at "23.3 percent" it stays liquid down to "-6°F" (about -21 C), and its "peak performance is
  obtained above 15°F. However, at temperatures below 20°F, most agencies will consider blending other products or
  brine additives" (Maine DOT, "Understanding Salt", June 2022). Laid before a storm, brine "works immediately as a
  barrier so that the precipitation doesn't stick to the road" and "can stay active on the road for several days";
  pre-wetted rock salt "clings to the road instead of bouncing off or being swept off by traffic", so "less salt being
  spread, saving money" (Peel). Colder days: "magnesium chloride or calcium chloride, both of which dissociate into
  three ions, lowering the solution's freezing point even further" (PBS). The cost: "Electrolytes increase the rate at
  which metals will rust, leading to rusty cars" and "Salt can leach into groundwater and contaminate rivers and lakes"
  (PBS). Scale: "Highway deicing accounted for about 37% of total salt consumed" in the U.S. (USGS Mineral Commodity
  Summaries 2026, data for 2025; leave out PBS's 2011 tonnage, it does not fit USGS). A chain (breaks marked "ours" are
  ours): A1, top-down drone at dusk, a truck lays neat wet stripes of brine on a dry highway as the first flakes fall.
  A2, black ice at dawn, cars creeping and sliding slowly on a glazed road, wide, no crash, no faces. A3, macro, grains
  of rock salt sink into ice and a ring of brine spreads, the ice turning to slush. A4 (ours, from Peel's line), dry
  salt from a spinner bounces off the frozen road and passing tyres sweep it into the ditch. A5, brine sprayed onto the
  salt as it leaves the spinner, the wet grains staying where they land. A6 (ours), snow falls on a bare road and packs
  into a crust that a plow blade skates over. B1, snow lands on the brine stripes and never bonds, the plow peels it off
  clean. B2, a bitter night far below 15 F, salt grains lying on the ice doing nothing. B3, a liquid chloride additive
  poured into the brine tank, and the ice gives way again. B4, a tease of whichever build follows. B5, a convoy of plows
  side by side on a night highway, then a wet black road at sunrise. Line 12: "23.3%" (or "37%", the roads' share of
  U.S. salt). Line 13: below about minus 21 C even the strongest brine freezes, so on the coldest nights salt alone
  stops working. B6: funny slow motion, a man seen from behind shakes a tiny salt shaker over a whole frozen driveway.
  Check before a line uses it: Peel says the dissolving salt releases "heat that melts the ice"; leave that out until a
  chemistry source is read (rock salt dissolving is usually described as taking in a little heat, calcium chloride as
  giving it off). The share of dry salt that bounces off (UConn's brine fact sheet refuses fetches) and beet-juice
  additives were not read. No brand names on trucks, no faces. Free video ends Nov 28, so if the main chat wants this
  build, its pair has to run by then.
  [PBS NewsHour, Jan 18 2011](https://www.pbs.org/newshour/science/why-does-salt-melt-road-ice),
  [Peel Region](https://peelregion.ca/transportation/road-maintenance/winter-operations/anti-icing-pre-wetting),
  [Maine DOT, June 2022](https://www1.maine.gov/mdot/mlrc/docs/technical/2023/UnderstandingSalt.pdf),
  [USGS MCS 2026, salt](https://pubs.usgs.gov/periodicals/mcs2026/mcs2026-salt.pdf)
