## 2026-11-27 03:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Build candidates (new to these notes; neither is in LINEUP or episodes.yml)
- A quartz watch (no watch build yet; the nearest is build 64, Big Ben's clock), an everyday build with no date
  peg: the strangest true picture is a crystal tuning fork inside the watch that rings 32,768 times a second. How:
  the crystal is "precisely cut and shaped like a tuning fork", and "if you squeeze a quartz crystal, it generates a
  tiny electric voltage" (Explain That Stuff); it "oscillates at a
  frequency of 32,768 Hz" (Epson); "32,768 is two raised to the 15th power", so a "chain of 'flip-flop' circuit, 15
  bits long, will reach 1 Hz at the 15th bit", and 32,768 is "The smallest power of two that is greater than 20,000
  Hz" (SJX, an engineer's piece of Feb 2022; that this keeps the hum above our hearing is his framing, so a script
  checks it before saying so); a "step motor" "moves the hand by one index per second" (Epson). First on a shelf:
  "In 1927, Warren Marrison, a Canadian engineer working at Bell Telephone Laboratories, developed a clock that kept
  time using the vibrations of a quartz crystal", and quartz clocks were "more accurate than any mechanical
  timekeeper, gaining or losing only a second in three years" (Smithsonian, On Time); Guinness names Marrison and
  J. W. Horton in New York, "first shown to the public at an academic conference in October 1927", with a 50,000 Hz
  crystal. First on a wrist: the Quartz Astron 35SQ, sold in Tokyo on Dec 25 1969 for "450,000 yen, around the same
  price as some of the popular cars of the time" (Seiko museum; ETHW: "$1,250"), with a "Daily difference ±0.2
  seconds" and a "monthly difference ±5 seconds" when "the daily difference of mechanical watches was ±20 seconds"
  (Seiko museum); its IEEE Milestone was dedicated on Nov 25 2004 (ETHW). Careful: the Astron itself ran at "8,192
  Hz", and "32,768 Hz became the standard for later quartz timepieces" (Seiko museum), so the 32,768 line is about
  today's watches. Breaks and fixes on the pages read: "the quartz vibrates at a slightly different frequency at
  different temperatures and pressures" (Explain That Stuff); Epson "developed a temperature compensating device
  using a variable capacitor" and "devised a method of suspending the oscillator inside a vacuum capsule" (that the
  capsule answers the pressure is our link); crystals creep with age, "quartz drifting or ageing", so fine movements
  use "quartz crystals aged for at least three months" (SJX); and a movement "may stop without warning once the
  battery goes flat" (SJX). Chain: a 1960s station where the passengers' wristwatches all disagree with the platform
  clock (A2) → squeeze quartz and it makes a voltage, give it a voltage and it rings at its own steady rate (A3) →
  32,768 swings a second, far too fast for gears to count (A4, ours) → fifteen halvings down to one tick a second for
  a stepping motor (A5) → a watch left on a sunny dashboard or out in the snow drifts (A6) → the sealed capsule and
  the temperature trimmer (B1) → a brand-new crystal still creeps as it ages (B2) → crystals aged for months before
  they go in (B3) → Tokyo, December 1969, a gold wristwatch in a shop window and people seen from behind crowding to
  look (B5, no maker's name on screen). A1: macro of an opened watch, a quartz tuning fork the size of a grain of
  rice in its tiny metal capsule, blurring as it rings. B6: funny slow motion, a man seen from behind holds his
  stopped watch to his ear as the train doors close in front of him. Stat line: "±5 SECONDS" a month (against ±20 a
  day). Kept out: the faces of Marrison, Horton and the team "headed by Tsuneya Nakamura" (ETHW), brand names on
  screen (SJX's "as much as a Toyota Corolla" becomes "as much as a car"), and the Swiss watch crisis (not on the
  pages read).
  [Explain That Stuff](https://www.explainthatstuff.com/quartzclockwatch.html),
  [Epson, the first quartz watch](https://corporate-origin.epson.biz/en/technology/overview/wearable/quartz-watch.html),
  [SJX, Feb 2022](https://watchesbysjx.com/2022/02/perspectives-quartz-timekeeping.html),
  [Seiko Museum, the IEEE award](https://museum.seiko.co.jp/en/seiko_history/milestone/milestone_03/),
  [Seiko Museum, accuracy](https://museum.seiko.co.jp/en/knowledge/Quartz01/),
  [ETHW, Milestone](https://ethw.org/Milestones:Electronic_Quartz_Wristwatch%2C_1969),
  [Smithsonian, On Time](https://americanhistory.si.edu/ontime/expanding/seconds.html),
  [Guinness, first quartz clock](https://guinnessworldrecords.com/world-records/754830-first-quartz-clock)
- The Seven Mile Bridge in the Florida Keys, a sea bridge with a trend hook this week (see Trends below) and a live
  story: the state wants to replace it. The old one, built "1909- 1912" (Library of Congress HAER record; WLRN in 2018
  says 1908-1910: check), carried the railroad to Key West and was "acclaimed as the longest bridge in the world"
  (HAER); "After 1935, the bridges and railroad right-of-way were sold to the state and the bridges were converted for
  highway use" (WLRN 2018), and the old rails were "recycled, painted white, and used as guardrails" (Wikipedia). The
  new one opened on May 24 1982: "440 spans" of "precast, prestressed concrete sections" in a box girder that rises
  "in an arc" to a "65-foot-high (20 m) clearance for boat passage" (Wikipedia; the old one had a swing span over
  Moser Channel, since removed). The breaks: the old bridge's steel beams "rusted out after decades of exposure to
  saltwater" (WLRN 2018); "Unsupported sections were added in 1935 to widen it for vehicular traffic", they "began to
  sag", and the stretch to Pigeon Key closed to cars in 2008 (Wikipedia); the 1982 bridge stands in "an extremely
  corrosive environment, surrounded by salt water", had "6 major rehabilitations through 2019", and the state's
  engineers said in May 2024 that it "should be fully replaced instead of continuously repaired like it has been",
  the new one "built to last at least 75 years" (WLRN, May 17 2024; the study was "projected to wrap up sometime in
  the winter of 2026", so a script checks where it stands). Proof: the old bridge "reopened in January 2022" for
  walkers and cyclists (Wikipedia; its cost is $77 million in Wikipedia and $38 million in WLRN 2018: check). Chain:
  islands strung across open sea and a railroad that wants Key West (A2) → piers and girders marching out across the
  water (A3) → the 1935 hurricane wrecks the line (A4; no deaths on screen or in the script) → the rail bridges become
  a road, the rails turned into white guardrails (A5) → the added road sections sag and the steel rusts in the salt
  (A6) → a new bridge of 440 precast concrete spans with a hump high enough for boats (B1) → salt eats the new one
  too, six big repairs (B2) → build the next one to last 75 years (B3) → walkers and cyclists on the restored old
  bridge at sunset, the new one alongside (B5). A1: a slow aerial at sunrise over two bridges side by side across
  turquoise water, the old one with a gap where its middle used to swing. B6: funny slow motion, a pelican lands in a
  cyclist's handlebar basket on the old bridge. Kept out: the hurricanes' dead, faces (the railroad's backer and its
  engineers), and anything from the game.
  [LOC, HAER FL-2](https://loc.gov/pictures/item/fl0293),
  [WLRN, Sep 10 2018](https://www.wlrn.org/news/2018-09-10/the-century-old-bridge-thats-more-than-just-a-transportation-route),
  [WLRN, May 17 2024](https://www.wlrn.org/transportation/2024-05-17/seven-mile-bridge-florida-keys-replacement-repair-fdot),
  [Wikipedia, Seven Mile Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Mile_Bridge)

  angles about 30 degrees and steeper" (Park City); crossing the fall line "can be risky", and an operator says "you
  take off sliding downhill and won't be able to climb back up" (KPCW, Apr 14 2024); so "The free end of the cable is
  anchored at the top of a slope" (Park City), to "a metal pole, or ... another snow cat" (KPCW), with "cable lengths
  of up to 1,200 metres and a tractive force of up to 4.8 tonnes" (Wikipedia), and the winch "enables the snowcat to
  control its descents" (Park City). A maker's own claim: "In 1985, PistenBully was the first snow grooming company to
  introduce a self-contained winch", two machines for Mammoth Mountain's 1985-86 winter; Mammoth now has "82 different
  winch picks" (PistenBully, Nov 7 2025; the brand stays off screen and out of the script). Timing: groomers go out
  "during the night time after the close of the ski area" with "visual or acoustic signals" (Wikipedia) and work "all
  night until just before our lifts start spinning at 9:00 am" (Park City). Proof: Deer Valley's "50 groomers who work
  13-person shifts" (drivers, not machines) put in "a combined 182 hours of grooming every night consuming 1,000
  gallons of diesel" (KPCW). Chain: a slope scraped into
  ice and heaps by a day of skiers (A2, ours) → wide tracks that float on the snow (A3) → a blade alone leaves loose
  lumps (A4, ours) → the spinning tiller and the comb leave corduroy (A5) → on a steep face the machine slides (A6) →
  the cable anchored at the top (B1) → a taut cable across a dark slope (B2, ours: no page read reports an accident,
  so keep it a picture of the risk) → work after the lifts close, lit up and signalling (B3) → Deer Valley's night
  (B5). A1: at night a groomer under its own floodlights hangs on one taut steel cable on a steep white face, leaving
  corduroy behind it. B6: funny slow motion, a carpet just vacuumed into neat stripes and the dog trots straight across
  it. Stat line: "1,200 METERS" of cable. Kept out: makers' names and logos, drivers' faces (cabs seen from outside).
  [Wikipedia, Snow grooming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_grooming),
  [Park City, Mar 10 2023](https://www.parkcitymountain.com/blog/science-of-grooming.aspx),
  [PistenBully, Nov 7 2025](https://www.pistenbully.com/en-US/news/2025/winch-40),
  [KPCW, Apr 14 2024](https://www.kpcw.org/ski-resorts/2024-04-14/how-deer-valley-resort-snow-groomers-lay-down-the-corduroy)
- An icebreaker that goes backwards, and one that goes sideways (a winter sea build). An icebreaking bow is "designed
  to break the ice by bending it under the ship's weight" (Wikipedia, Double acting ship). The surprise: "In the late
  1800s, captains ... discovered that sometimes it was easier to break through ice by running their vessels astern"
  ("Although not known at the time", the propellers' flow "lowered the ice resistance"); the cost: "the steering
  ability of a ship is greatly reduced when running astern" (Wikipedia). The fix: a propeller pod that turns all the
  way round, so the ship steers going astern; model tests in 1994 gave a "40–50% reduction in ice resistance due to
  lubricating effect of propeller-induced water flow" (Wikipedia), and "Propellers mill the underwater part of the
  ice ridge to open a passage" (ABB, Sep 14 2021). Proof: the first double acting tanker, Tempera, delivered in 2002
  (sister Mastera 2003; 106,000 tonnes deadweight, one 16 MW pod): "The ships can break level ice up to 1 metre (3.3
  ft) thick at 3 knots" stern first (Wikipedia; Marine Insight's "2 knots in 1mm thick ice" is a misprint: don't use).
  The second twist: escorting ships "up to 40 metres (130 ft) wide" took "two conventional icebreakers with a beam of
  23–25 metres", so the same design centre (MARC) drew "an asymmetrical, triangle-shaped vessel with three azimuth
  thrusters in the 'corners'" that pushes at "a 50-degree angle of attack—almost sideways—in ice" to "generate a
  50-metre (55 yd) channel in 0.6-metre (2.0 ft) ice"; the 76.4 m ship was "completed in 2014" at Arctech Helsinki
  Shipyard and showed sideways icebreaking in 2015 trials (Wikipedia, Oblique icebreaker, which also notes "doubts
  about the ability of the relatively small vessel to break a wide channel in ice", with no answer given: check).
  Chain: a frozen sea with a ship stuck fast (A2, ours) → a bow that bends the ice under the ship's weight (A3) → a
  thick ridge stops the bow dead (A4, ours) → going astern, the propellers wash and chew the ice away (A5) → a ship
  going backwards can hardly steer (A6) → the pod that turns all the way round (B1) → a ship far wider than the
  icebreaker's channel (B2) → the triangle ship going sideways (B3) → a big tanker reversing steadily through
  metre-thick ice (B5). A1: from the air, a squat triangle-shaped ship moving sideways across white sea ice, a wide
  black channel opening behind it. B6: funny slow motion, a man cannot push his loaded shopping trolley through deep
  snow in a car park, turns round and pulls it backwards with ease, then slips. Stat line: "50 METERS" of open water.
  Kept out: the ships' owners and the oil route they were built for (a state ministry and an Arctic trade: no
  politics), crews' faces, makers' names.
  [Wikipedia, Double acting ship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_acting_ship),
  [Wikipedia, Oblique icebreaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oblique_icebreaker),
  [ABB, Sep 14 2021](https://new.abb.com/news/detail/82273/menu-login-button),
  [Marine Insight](https://www.marineinsight.com/types-of-ships/what-are-double-acting-tankers/)

## 2026-11-27 05:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Build candidate (the 18:00 entry's Dec 3 lead, now read; not in LINEUP or episodes.yml)
- A self-balancing scooter, for Thu Dec 3, 25 years since the Segway was unveiled (say "self-balancing scooter"; the
  brand only as a name in a caption, no logo; its inventor off screen). The trick: "walking is actually a form of
  controlled falling", and the machine keeps its wheels "directly below the user's center of gravity" (Scientific
  American, "Power Walker", undated, written "five years after" the 2001 debut); "the rider just leans slightly
  forward or backward" (HowStuffWorks, undated). It has "five gyroscopic sensors, though it only needs three" ("The
  extra sensors add redundancy") and "two tilt sensors filled with electrolyte fluid", and "the controller boards
  check the position sensors about 100 times per second" (HowStuffWorks; SciAm: "100 times a second"). Two boards:
  "if one board breaks down, the other will take over all functions so that the system can notify the rider of a
  failure and shut down gracefully"; "a pair of rechargeable" batteries; "Top speed: 12.5 miles per hour (20 kph)";
  it turns by spinning "one wheel faster than the other, or ... the wheels in opposite directions" (HowStuffWorks).
  A real break: on Sep 26 2003 "Approximately 6,000" were recalled because "particularly when the batteries are near
  the end of charge, some Segway HTs may not deliver enough power, allowing the rider to fall", for instance when
  "the rider speeds up abruptly, encounters an obstacle, or continues to ride after receiving a low-battery alert"
  (three falls reported, one head injury that needed stitches); the remedy was "a free software upgrade" (CPSC alert
  03-553, Justia's copy; NPR's short page agrees). What the upgrade changed is not said, so a script says only "a
  software fix". A second break: the handlebar-less copies ("Segways without handlebars", "one of the hottest items"
  of the 2015 holidays: All About Circuits, Mar 4 2016), with a motor and tilt sensors in each wheel and a gyroscope
  "inside the foot platform" (Live Science, Mar 25 2016). On Jul 6 2016 "About 501,000" from 10 firms were recalled:
  "The lithium-ion battery packs ... can overheat, posing a risk of the products smoking, catching fire and/or
  exploding" ("at least 99" incidents), and CPSC had looked into "more than 60 hoverboard fires in more than 20
  states" with "more than $2 million in property damage". They were "made and sold without a safety standard in
  place"; the fix is UL 2272, "testing requirements ... that address the electrical system and battery pack problems"
  (CPSC's recall page and its chairman's statement, both Jul 6 2016; the fall hazard was still open then). The end:
  production stopped "after mid-July" 2020, about 140,000 sold "since the company was founded", "more often used on
  city sightseeing tours" (Lonely Planet, Jul 2 2020); SciAm had "fewer than 24,000" five years in, at "$4,000 to
  $5,500 apiece".
  Chain: two wheels side by side tip a rider onto his nose (A2, ours) → sensors feel the lean 100 times a second and
  the motors roll the wheels back under him, falling and catching like walking (A3) → a sensor or board fails mid-ride
  (A4, ours: a picture of the risk) → five gyros where three would do and a second board that takes over and stops
  gently (A5) → a nearly flat battery, not enough power to catch him (A6, the 2003 recall) → a software fix for every
  machine (B1) → a handlebar-less board smoking and catching fire in a dark living room (B2) → a battery pack tested in
  a lab chamber (B3) → a line of riders on a city sightseeing tour, people watching (B5). A1: at dawn in an empty city
  square a rider on two big wheels set side by side leans forward like a falling tree, and the wheels roll under him
  and catch him. B6: funny slow motion, a man balancing a broom upright on his palm chases it across a kitchen and
  crashes into the fridge. Stat line: "100 TIMES A SECOND". Kept out: the deaths in Lonely Planet's piece, the film,
  brand names on screen, riders' faces (from behind or in helmets); the famous backers' forecasts ("bigger than the
  PC", Lonely Planet; "cities will be built around it", the Crimson) only unattributed, if at all.
  The unveiling: on "Good Morning America" on Dec 3 2001 (This Day in Tech History; the Harvard Crimson, Dec 4 2001:
  "Code-named Ginger", a consumer version that "will cost around $3,000" in late 2002, "about 12 miles per hour", "up
  to 15 miles on a single electric charge", and a "$250,000 advance for a book about Ginger without even knowing what
  the invention was"; its "100 times per minute" is a slip for per second). Neither page names the place, so a script
  does not.
  Timing (our count): with builds 100-105 posting Nov 28 and six a day, Dec 3 falls on about builds 130-135, so the
  run that appends those LINEUP entries can put it there; that is after the free Seedance video ends, so it is a v2
  or stills build unless its pair runs before Nov 28 ends. Not read: UPI's and IEEE Spectrum's pages (both waited for
  an approval) and NPR's 2016 and 2020 pages (refused by robots.txt).
  [Scientific American, Power Walker](https://www.scientificamerican.com/article/power-walker),
  [HowStuffWorks, How Segways Work](https://science.howstuffworks.com/engineering/civil/ginger1.htm),
  [CPSC alert 03-553 (Justia), Sep 26 2003](https://recalls.justia.com/operating-equipment/human-transporters/03-553),
  [NPR, Sep 26 2003](https://www.npr.org/2003/09/26/1447863/safety-concerns-prompt-segway-recall),
  [CPSC, recall 16-218, Jul 6 2016](https://www.cpsc.gov/Recalls/2016/self-balancing-scooters-hoverboards-recalled-by-10-firms),
  [CPSC, chairman's statement, Jul 6 2016](https://www.cpsc.gov/about-cpsc/chairman/elliot-f-kaye/statements/chairmans-hoverboard-press-statement),
  [Live Science, Mar 25 2016](https://www.livescience.com/53046-hoverboard-scooter-how-it-works.html),
  [All About Circuits, Mar 4 2016](https://www.allaboutcircuits.com/news/hoverboards-how-do-they-work/),
  [Lonely Planet, Jul 2 2020](https://www.lonelyplanet.com/articles/segway-ending-production-scooter),

## 2026-11-27 06:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Build candidates (new to these notes; neither is in LINEUP or episodes.yml)
- Airliner de-icing, the orange spray and then the green one (a winter build for the holiday flights; ep11 built the
  airplane, not this). Why it matters: frost "similar to medium or coarse sandpaper" "can reduce maximum wing lift by
  as much as 30% and increase lift-induced drag by 40%", and "the stall angle will decrease" (NASA Glenn, A Pilot's
  Guide to Ground Icing, upd. Sep 27 2016; AOPA, Oct 4 2021, gives the same figures); contamination on the leading
  edge can let "the wing stalling angle to be reached prior to any indication by the stall warning" (Transport Canada
  TP 14052, Issue 10, Mar 2026). The two fluids: Type I is "a mix of glycol and water and has a distinctive orange
  colour" that "breaks the bond between frost, ice or snow and the wings"; Type IV is "an anti-icing fluid that stops
  new ice or snow from sticking to the plane" and "is bright green in colour" (Toronto Pearson, Dec 13 2021). "Type I
  must always be applied heated and diluted" and, "the least viscous (thinnest) of fluids, it has the shortest HOTs"
  (holdover times); the thickening agents "correspond to longer HOTs" but make the fluids "more resistant to shearing
  off the aircraft during the take-off roll" (NASA), and "Thickened fluids, such as Types II & IV, are aerodynamically
  designed for 'high take-off speed' aircraft" (TP 14052). The routine: "Two step deicing/anti-icing is generally used
  when the aircraft is contaminated and when precipitation is active", the second coat going on "before any freezing
  of the ADF occurs" (the first fluid), "typically within 3 minutes"; the holdover clock starts at the "Time noted at
  the start of anti-icing fluid application" (TP 14052); "Ultimately, the anti-icing fluid will fail" (NASA), so crews
  "Perform a pre-take-off contamination inspection just prior to take-off" (TP 14052). What the rules say once the
  time has run out was read only in a summary (TP 14052, 11.1.7): check it before a script says it. Proof: Toronto
  Pearson's Central Deicing Facility, "the largest and most technologically advanced centralized deicing facility in
  the world" (the airport's page, Dec 13 2021, in its description; ESE Magazine's design-era article: "will be the
  largest facility of its kind in the world"), six pads on 65 acres, "200 staff members and 46 deicing trucks", "500
  aircraft in a single day" (Pearson); at one time "six Class E aircraft (e.g. B747), or twelve Class C aircraft (eg.
  B737, A320)" (ESE) or "up to 12 widebody aircraft or 24 narrowbody jets" (Bolt Flight, Feb 13 2026): say "a dozen
  jets at once". The pads slope so the spent fluid runs "directly into special drains" and "underground storage tanks"
  of "a combined volume of 15 million litres", where it is "measured, tested and recycled for use in other markets"
  (Pearson; Bolt Flight's "19 million liters" disagrees: use the airport's figure). Last winter: "approximately 16,800
  aircraft" by mid-February, "nearly 17,000" (Bolt Flight, a third-party mid-season count; no final figure found).
  Chain: snow settles on a jet's wings at the gate (A2) → a heated orange spray melts it and blasts it off (A3) → snow
  keeps falling on the clean wing while the jet waits in line (A4, ours) → a thick green coat that holds the new snow
  off (A5) → the coat gives out if the wait is too long (A6, NASA's "will fail") → a last look at the wing just before
  takeoff (B1; who looks and from where is not in the pages read: check) → orange glycol running off into the snow and
  a creek (B2, ours: a picture of the problem) → sloped pads, drains and tanks that catch it for recycling (B3) →
  Pearson's six pads at night in a snowstorm, a line of jets waiting their turn (B5). A1: at night in falling snow a
  jet sits under floodlights while two crane trucks hose it down in clouds of orange steam, then coat it bright green.
  B6: funny slow motion, a man scrapes his frozen windshield clean and the snow on the roof slides down over him. Stat
  line: "30%" of the lift (NASA) or "500 A DAY" (Pearson). Kept out: airline names and liveries (plain white tails),
  fluid brand names, the 2002 Birmingham accident on NASA's page (deaths), crews' faces (hoods, from behind).
  [NASA Glenn, ground icing fluids](https://aircrafticing.grc.nasa.gov/2_5_2_1.html),
  [NASA Glenn, frost and lift](https://aircrafticing.grc.nasa.gov/2_1_2_1.html),
  [AOPA, Oct 4 2021](https://www.aopa.org/news-and-media/all-news/2021/october/04/training-tip-rough-stuff),
  [Transport Canada, TP 14052](https://tc.canada.ca/en/aviation/publications/guidelines-aircraft-ground-icing-operations-tp-14052),
  [Toronto Pearson, Dec 13 2021](https://www.torontopearson.com/en/whats-happening/stories/aircraft-deicing-2021),
  [ESE Magazine](https://esemag.com/archives/central-de-icing-facility-at-torontos-airport-largest-facility-of-its-kind-in-the-world/),
  [Bolt Flight, Feb 13 2026](https://boltflight.com/toronto-pearson-sets-historic-winter-benchmark-with-nearly-17000-aircraft-de-iced-in-2025-2026-season/)
- The Dunedin of 1882, the sailing ship that carried the first frozen meat across the world (a "first" with a real
  voyage as its proof: the start of the cold chain). The problem: "the shipment of livestock from New Zealand to
  England was prohibitively expensive" and canned meat was popular in the Pacific islands but "less so in England",
  while in the UK "the rapidly expanding population had outrun the supply of local meat, resulting in rapid increases
  in prices" (Wikipedia, Dunedin (1874 ship)). The machine: a "Bell-Coleman compression refrigeration machine, cooling
  the entire hold", "steam-powered" on "3 tons of coal a day" (Wikipedia); "The compressor compressed the warm air and
  when it was released... it came out at a very low temperature" (RNZ, May 27 2022, quoting a historian); the hold was
  kept "22 degrees celsius below the outside temperature" (NZHistory). The carcasses were "sewn into calico bags and
  frozen" on board (Wikipedia): "There were no freezing works or cool stores in those days" (a 1932 recollection
  quoted by the NZ Herald, Feb 8 2025). "About 5000 carcasses were on board the Dunedin when it sailed on 15 February"
  1882 from Port Chalmers (NZHistory); Wikipedia's tally is "4331 mutton, 598 lamb and 22 pig carcasses, 250 kegs of
  butter" (the Herald has 4,311 and RNZ "5000 lamb carcasses": use Wikipedia's, attributed). The breaks: before it
  sailed "the crankshaft of the compressor broke", "causing the loss of the 643 sheep carcasses" (Wikipedia; the
  Herald and RNZ give other counts); "Sparks from the compressor's boiler created a fire hazard" (Wikipedia) and
  "occasionally the sails would catch on fire" (RNZ); in the tropics "the ship was caught on one tack for a long time
  and the cold air wasn't moving in the hold. So the temperatures were rising" (RNZ), and "Captain John Whitson
  crawled inside and sawed extra air holes, almost freezing to death" (NZHistory; RNZ: he went down "on a rope on a
  ventilation shaft" and was pulled out by it). The proof: "The Dunedin arrived in London 98 days after setting sail"
  (Wikipedia; May 24 1882, RNZ) and "only one carcass was condemned" (NZHistory); John Swan and Sons, quoted by
  Wikipedia: "Directly the meat was placed on the market, its superiority over the Australian [frozen] meat struck
  us"; "Within five years, 172 shipments of frozen meat were sent from New Zealand to the United Kingdom" (Wikipedia),
  the trade that "became the cornerstone of New Zealand's 20th-century economy" (NZHistory). Chain: a hungry 1880s
  city with bare butchers' hooks, and green hills of sheep half a world away (A2) → a steam engine squeezes air and
  lets it out ice-cold into the hold (A3) → the crankshaft snaps in port and the first carcasses thaw (A4) → mended,
  it freezes the cargo hard in calico bags on board (A5, the repair is not described in the pages read: check) →
  sparks from the boiler set a sail alight (A6) → 22 degrees colder than the air outside, even near the equator (B1) →
  becalmed on one tack, the cold air stops moving and the hold warms (B2) → the captain, seen from behind, crawls down
  the air shaft on a rope and saws new air holes (B3) → London, May 1882, the frozen cargo is unloaded before watching
  crowds, one carcass condemned (B5). A1: a three-masted sailing ship on a blazing tropical sea with a small chimney
  smoking on its deck and white frost spilling out of its open hatch (whether the ship had a funnel and where the
  machine sat are not in the pages read: check before the prompt). B6: funny slow motion, a man in a heatwave opens
  his freezer and puts his whole head inside, eyes closed in bliss. Stat line: "98 DAYS" (or "22°C COLDER"). Pictures:
  Frederick Tudgay's "Dunedin, 1882" (Te Ara, from the Hocken Collections) is not free: "You may not use this item
  commercially" and "Permission of the Hocken Collections ... must be obtained" (DigitalNZ, record updated Nov 26
  2025). A free period still of the same port: Te Papa's "S.S. Ionic [1] at Port Chalmers" (Burton Brothers, about
  1884, an albumen print, O.000905), "This image has a rights statement of No Known Copyright Restrictions", with a
  Download button (another ship, not the Dunedin). Kept out: the ship's loss in 1890 with all on board, the captain's
  face, sheep in distress (the shots show cloth-wrapped cargo, not animals); "too many sheep" and "boiled down for
  tallow" are not in the pages read, so a script does not say them. Not read: the Lloyd's Register Foundation heritage
  page (its permission request timed out).
  [NZHistory, first shipment of frozen meat](https://nzhistory.govt.nz/first-shipment-of-frozen-meat-leaves-nz),
  [Wikipedia, Dunedin (1874 ship)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunedin_(1874_ship)),
  [NZ Herald, Feb 8 2025](https://www.nzherald.co.nz/the-country/news/pastures-past-new-zealands-first-frozen-meat-shipment-in-1882/MGWJDDCAFNGTXJHC7S36BDFICQ),
  [RNZ, May 27 2022](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/countrylife/551372/a-milestone-marked-for-meat-industry),
  [DigitalNZ, Dunedin 1882](https://digitalnz.org/records/31929550/dunedin-1882),
  [Te Papa, S.S. Ionic at Port Chalmers](https://collections.tepapa.govt.nz/object/195026)

Free picture sources (new to these notes)
- Te Papa, the Museum of New Zealand, Collections Online: images marked "no known copyright restrictions" are
  "downloadable for any use, any use at all" and credit "isn't mandatory"; others carry "CC BY-NC-ND" ("You can't make
  money from using the image"), so not for our channels (Te Papa blog, Jun 3 2014). On the site: tick "with
  downloadable images", then "Download Image" and "Download JPEG" (tips page); DigitalNZ's "Use Commercially" filter
  finds the free ones (a reply on the blog, Sep 15 2014). The count is from 2014 ("over 30,000 images downloadable,
  for free") and no size is given; the tips page also points re-users to "Buy or License", so take only items whose

## 2026-11-27 07:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Build candidate (new to these notes; not in LINEUP or episodes.yml)
- Float glass: every window floated on a lake of liquid metal (it fits the science-explainer pattern under Trends,
  whose top outlier this month was a TikTok about making glass from soil). The problem: plate glass was rolled, then
  ground, then polished, and even with the twin grinder "around 20% of the glass" was ground off "as sand which would
  end up in landfill" (Pilkington, Plate process); drawn sheet glass had surfaces "not as smooth or uniform as those of
  float glass" (Wikipedia, Float glass). The fix: glass from a melter at 1,500 °C flows onto molten tin "starting at
  1,100°C" and leaves "as a solid ribbon at 600°C", settling by itself at "a single equilibrium thickness of 6.8 mm"
  (Pilkington, step by step); the tin bath is "about 3–4 m wide, 50 m long, 6 cm deep" (Wikipedia; Smil: "4 to 8
  meters in width and up to 60 meters in length"). Breaks and fixes: tin turns to tin dioxide, "dross", that "adheres
  to the glass", so the bath sits under "a positive pressure protective atmosphere of nitrogen and hydrogen"
  (Wikipedia); "considerable stresses are developed in the ribbon as it cools", and with too much "the glass will break
  beneath the cutter" (Pilkington), so a lehr of about 100 m cools it "gradually so that it anneals without strain"
  (Wikipedia); one natural thickness is not enough, so "Top rollers positioned above the molten tin may be used to
  control both the thickness and the width" (Wikipedia), now "sub-millimetre to 25 mm" (Pilkington). Other flaws: "a
  bubble is not removed during refining, a sand grain refuses to melt, a tremor in the tin puts ripples into the glass
  ribbon" (Pilkington). The story: Alastair Pilkington "began experimenting in December 1952" (the company calls the
  washing-up tale "a myth"); "All pieces of glass which were made by the process between 1952 and 1959 were not
  perfect"; the first sheet made entirely by float came in "July 1958"; it was announced "On 20th January 1959" "after
  seven years of effort and £28 million" (Pilkington, Invention of float glass: its own sum, check it before a script
  says it); Wikipedia also credits Kenneth Bickerstaff and dates "Full scale profitable sales" to 1960. Proof: "float
  glass plants work nonstop for 11 to 15 years" (Vaclav Smil, IEEE Spectrum, Mar 30 2022), a line "may be nearly half
  a kilometre long", "around 500 glass float lines in the world" (Pilkington, undated: say "hundreds"). A reveal: under
  a shortwave UV lamp "The tin side will glow; the air side won't" (J Racenstein, a glazing supplier, read only in a
  summary: check the wording). Chain: giant wheels grind a glass plate in a slurry of sand in a 1950s works (A2) →
  glowing glass pours onto a mirror of molten tin and spreads flat by itself (A3) → grey dross skins the tin and spots
  the ribbon (A4) → the bath sealed under a gas blanket (A5) → the cooled ribbon cracks under the cutter (A6) → a long
  tunnel, the lehr, cools it slowly (B1) → every sheet comes out the same 6.8 mm (B2) → rollers above the tin set the
  thickness and width (B3) → a float line at night, the ribbon gliding out after years of nonstop running (B5). A1: in a
  dark factory a glowing orange river of glass slides onto a lake of liquid silver metal and stretches away into the
  distance, slow push-in. B6: funny slow motion, a man walks nose-first into a spotless glass door and squashes
  against it, his coffee sloshing. Stat line: "11 TO 15 YEARS" nonstop (Smil) or "6.8 MM" (Pilkington). Kept out:
  Smil's "about 1,050 °C" melt and "230 °C" tin bath (they clash with Pilkington's figures), company names and logos
  on screen, Sir Alastair's face. Adding it is the main chat's call; its Seedance jobs could only run by Nov 28.
  [Pilkington, Plate process](https://www.pilkington.com/en/gbl/about-us/pilkington-history/plate-process),
  [Pilkington, step by step](https://www.pilkington.com/en/global/knowledge-base/glass-technology/the-float-process/the-float-process-step-by-step),
  [Pilkington, Invention of float glass](https://www.pilkington.com/en-gb/uk/about/heritage/invention-of-float-glass),
  [Wikipedia, Float glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Float_glass),
  [Vaclav Smil, IEEE Spectrum, Mar 30 2022](https://spectrum.ieee.org/float-glass),
  [J Racenstein, the tin side](https://jracenstein.com/how-to-find-the-tin-side-of-float-glass-and-why-it-matters)

  "If an image includes an identifiable person, using the image for commercial purposes may infringe that person's

  build it opens (new to these notes): the LSST Camera, "the 3200-megapixel camera", "roughly the size of a small car
  and weighs around 3000 kilograms", with a front lens "over 1.5 meters (5 feet) across" and "201 individual
  custom-designed CCD sensors" (the camera page's picture caption says "189 square CCD chips": check) on a focal plane
  "so flat that its surface varies by no more than a tenth of the width of a human hair"; "it would take hundreds of
  ultra-high-definition TVs to display just one of its images at full size"; built at SLAC "After two decades of
  work", then "driven 2737 meters (8980 feet) up Cerro Pachón" (NOIRLab, Apr 3 2024). Its sensors are "kept extremely
  cold (about -100°C or -148°F) in order to limit the number of defective (bright) pixels"; six filters "30 in/75 cm
  across" share a carousel of five and are swapped "in less than two minutes"; it was "installed on the Simonyi Survey
  Telescope in March 2025" (Rubin, camera page). Breaks to build on: warm sensors and bright pixels (the cold), a
  focal plane that must stay flat (a tenth of a hair), six filters and room for five (the loader), the drive up the
  mountain. Not in the pages read: the telescope's speed and mirror size, the date of the first images. Kept out:
  people in the photos, the logo.

## 2026-11-27 08:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Build candidate (new to these notes; not in LINEUP or episodes.yml)
- A tower crane, the machine that makes itself taller (a sight in every growing city; it pairs with 100, the Jeddah
  Tower). The start: it stands on "a large concrete pad" poured "several weeks before the crane arrives", "30 feet by
  30 feet by 4 feet (10 x 10 x 1.3 meters)" and "400,000 pounds (182,000 kg)", gripped by "Large anchor bolts"; it
  comes "on 10 to 12 tractor-trailer rigs" and a mobile crane sets the jib and machinery on a first "40-foot
  (12-meter) mast", then "adds the counterweights" (HowStuffWorks, Marshall Brain, undated). Breaks and fixes: a load
  far out on the jib tips it, so "large concrete counter weights" sit on the short machinery arm ("20 tons"; the page's
  "16.3 metric tons" does not match: check) and two limit switches watch the hoist: one "makes sure that the load does
  not exceed 18 tonnes", the load moment switch "that the operator does not exceed the tonne-meter rating" (300
  tonne-meters, so "a maximum of 10.1 tonnes" at 30 m); the building outgrows it ("265 feet (80 meters)" standing
  alone), so it climbs: a "top climber or climbing frame" fits under the slewing unit, the crew hangs "a weight on the
  jib to balance the counterweight", "hydraulic rams in the top climber push the slewing unit up 20 feet (6 meters)",
  and the crane lifts "another 20-foot mast section into the gap"; higher still when "tied into the building"
  (HowStuffWorks). Wind: jibs "are designed to free slew when out of service to avoid high loadings", and a brake left
  partly on "could result in the jib or whole crane collapsing in strong winds" (HSE, FOD 5-2014, Oct 31 2014); a
  luffing jib must also be parked "at a safe out-of-service radius" or it "may not be able to weathervane freely in
  high winds" (HSE, FOD 2-2014, Feb 20 2014, after "the recent collapse of three luffing jib tower cranes in high
  winds"); "additional wind area" from add-ons "may also prevent an out-of-service crane from free slewing downwind"
  (HSE, CON2-2018, Dec 2018). The end, the best question: "how do they get the crane down when the building is
  finished?": "a rooftop crane lifts the tower parts down, then a smaller crane lifts that one down and so on", and a
  rooftop derrick then takes itself apart into pieces of at most "2.2 x 1.1 metres and a weight of 1,000 kgs"
  (Vertikal, Nov 16 2006; it does not say how those go down: do not claim the lifts); on the ground "the crane
  disassembles its own mast and then smaller cranes disassemble the rest" (HowStuffWorks). History: Germany, 1910
  ("the first tower crane in 1910"; ENR credits both Wolff and Gottlob Gobel); a "quick-erecting crane in 1949" with "a
  16-meter jib and 1,000-kg capacity"; "the first self-climbing tower crane" in 1956 (ENR, Aug 20 2008). Proof, Gulf:
  three tower cranes served the Burj Khalifa up to level 156 and "crane operators worked at heights of more than 700
  metres" (Emirates 24/7, Jan 3 2010, read only in its page summary: check); its 143 m, 450 t spire was put together
  inside the tower from 5 m sections and lifted 89 m by strand jacks (KHL, Euan Youdale, Feb 15 2010). Chain: a
  building to rise higher than any truck crane reaches (A2) → the giant pad and anchor bolts, a truck crane sets the
  first mast (A3) → a heavy load runs out to the jib's tip and the crane leans (A4) → concrete counterweights on the
  short arm, the hoist stops at its limit (A5) → the floors rise past the cab (A6) → rams push the top up 6 m and a new
  mast section slides into the gap (B1) → a storm hits a jib held tight by its brake, the steel groans (B2) → the brake
  let go, the jib swings round with the wind like a weather vane (B3) → a skyline at dusk, every crane turned the same
  way in the wind, or a rooftop crane lowering the big one piece by piece (B5). A1: above the clouds at dawn, the whole
  top of a tower crane rises on hydraulic rams and leaves a gap in its own tower, then lifts a new steel section into
  the gap, slow push-in. B6: funny slow motion, a man on a windy beach clings to an open beach umbrella that swings
  round with every gust and drags him across the sand. Stat line: "6 METRES" a climb or "182,000 KG" of pad
  (HowStuffWorks). Kept out: crane collapses with victims, brand names and logos on screen, real faces. Adding it is
  the main chat's call; its Seedance jobs could only run by Nov 28.
  [HowStuffWorks, How Tower Cranes Work](https://science.howstuffworks.com/transport/engines-equipment/tower-crane.htm/printable),
  [HowStuffWorks, How Do Tower Cranes Grow?](https://science.howstuffworks.com/transport/engines-equipment/tower-crane4.htm),
  [HSE, FOD 5-2014](https://www.hse.gov.uk/safetybulletins/tower-crane-slew-brakes.htm),
  [HSE, FOD 2-2014](https://www.hse.gov.uk/safetybulletins/luffing-jib-tower-cranes.htm),
  [HSE, CON2-2018](https://hse.gov.uk/safetybulletins/tower-crane-slew-brakes-release.htm),
  [Vertikal, Nov 16 2006](https://vertikal.net/en/news/story/3253/new-tower-crane-derrick-from-liebherr),
  [ENR, Aug 20 2008](https://www.enr.com/articles/29387-cranes-enabled-faster-safer-construction-of-tall-buildings),
  [Emirates 24/7, Jan 3 2010](https://www.emirates247.com/eb247/companies-markets/construction/a-complex-process-2010-01-04-1.5910),

Timing and a candidate build (new to these notes: no Marconi line yet)
- Sat Dec 12 2026 is 125 years since the first radio signal heard across the Atlantic, "the three dots – the letter
  'S' in Morse" (Oxford's History of Science Museum), "pip, pip, pip" (Heritage NL), sent from Poldhu in Cornwall to
  Signal Hill in St. John's. The chain, from IEEE's two milestone pages unless marked: the problem, "It was generally
  believed that the curvature of the earth made this impossible" (A2, Oxford); "about 400 wires suspended in an
  inverted cone from a 200-foot circle of 20, 200 feet high masts" (A3), flattened by "a wind storm on 17 September
  1901" (A4); "two masts, each a hundred and seventy five feet high" holding "sixty vertical wires" in a fan (A5);
  the Cape Cod antenna "blew down in a hurricane" on "26 November" (A6), so Newfoundland got a receiver only, landed
  "6 December", its wire lifted by kites and balloons (B1); "the balloons failed and one of the kites was blown away"
  (B2, Oxford); "The second kite, which trailed 500 feet of antenna wire, stayed up long enough", with "a telephone
  earpiece connected to the receiver" (B3); the diary, "Sigs. at 12:30, 1:10 and 2:20.", and "the only witness was
  George Kemp" (B5); two months later the signals worked "a Morse inker in the presence of witnesses", "on board the
  Philadelphia" at "over 2,000 miles" (Oxford). Closer: it led "Arthur Kennelly and Oliver Heaviside to suggest" a
  charged layer high in the air, the ionosphere, that bends the waves round the Earth. Honest caveats: "the signals
  were too weak to operate an automatic recorder", and "recent historians have suggested that Marconi picked up a
  high-frequency harmonic on his un-tuned receiver", so the script says he heard it, not that he proved it.
  Distance: IEEE "more than 2,000 miles", Heritage NL "1700 miles"; our great-circle sum, Poldhu to Signal Hill, is
  about 3,400 km (2,100 miles), so "2,000 MILES" for the stat line. A1: a kite in a winter gale over a sea cliff,
  trailing a thin copper wire down into a stone hut, slow push-in. B6: funny slow motion, a man on a wobbling chair
  holds his phone up to the ceiling for one bar of signal. No event for Dec 12 2026 found yet: last year's (the
  124th) was at the Johnson Geo Centre in St. John's with the Marconi Radio Club of Newfoundland and the Poldhu
  Amateur Radio Club (NTV, Dec 11 2025); the Lizard station's 125th ran as GB4LWS through January 2026 (ARNewsline,
  Jan 8 2026). Kept out: faces (his and his team's), company names and logos, the patent fights. Dec 12 is far past
  LINEUP's last idea (114), so it would need a dated slot, made after the free video ends (v2 look or free stills).
  The main chat's call.

## 2026-11-27 11:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

A candidate build (new to these notes: no floating-bridge line yet)
- The first train on a floating bridge: Seattle's 2 Line "opened to passengers Saturday morning", Mar 28 2026, on the
  I-90 floating bridge across Lake Washington: "It's a world first. No one has ever run light rail on a floating
  bridge before." (KUOW, Mar 28 2026; FOX 13 the same day). The chain, sources marked: "The lake was too deep, and its
  bottom was too mushy" for a bridge on piers (A2, History.com); so a road on "22 floating bolted-together pontoons"
  of hollow concrete held by anchor cables, opened Jul 2 1940 (A3; History.com, HistoryLink, Wikipedia). Break: on
  Sun Nov 25 1990, Thanksgiving weekend, that bridge sank. Its renovation blasted the sidewalks off with water that
  "could not be allowed to flow into Lake Washington", so the water was kept in the pontoons and "The watertight doors
  for the pontoons were therefore removed"; a storm filled them, and "It sank when one pontoon filled and dragged the
  rest down, because they were cabled together", a "2,790-foot (850 m) section", "captured on film and shown on live
  TV", and "No one was hurt" (it was closed for the works); "A dozen anchoring cables" of the new twin bridge beside it
  "were severed" (A4, Wikipedia; HistoryLink: "six-foot-high holes" cut in the pontoons; the storm is on Thanksgiving
  Day in History.com and Nov 22-24 in Wikipedia, so say "that holiday weekend"). Rebuilt, it reopened Sep 12 1993 (A5,
  Wikipedia). The train's problem: the lake rises and falls "2 feet annually, requiring anchor cable adjustments" (UW
  CEE, May 18 2026) and the deck takes "as many as six dynamic movements", "yaw, pitch, roll, and surge" among them
  (Metro Magazine, Apr 2018), so rails cannot simply run from the shore onto it (A6). Fix: track bridges at the joints,
  Metro's "eight production track bridges" for the "four expansion joints" of the UW thesis (2013; two tracks each, our
  reading): two box girders ("wings") on curved "uni-directional friction pendulum bearings", 17 bearer bars under the
  rails and sliding rail joints, no springs or dampers (Metro, paraphrased); about a quarter of one was tested in UW's
  lab (B1, thesis). Break: in 2019 "thousands of concrete 'plinths' supporting the tracks" turned out "poorly built" and
  were torn out, which pushed the opening to 2026 (B2, KUOW); poured again in Sep 2024 (B3, UW CEE caption). Proof: an
  unpowered train "pushed and then towed across" in May 2025, a four-car train in "livewire testing" on Oct 8 2025 (UW
  CEE, FOX 13), then opening day (B5). Stat line: "2 FEET" a year, or Bellevue to Chinatown-International District in
  "about 20 minutes" (KUOW); "55 miles per hour" is Metro's 2018 figure (check with Sound Transit). Closer: sensors in
  the pontoons now follow the bridge "down to millimeters of accuracy" (UW CEE). A1: a train gliding across a lake at
  dawn on a road that lies flat on the water, slow push-in. B6: funny slow motion, someone on a pool float carries a
  brimful cup of coffee as a wave rolls under. To check before a script: that the train runs on the 1989 twin (the one
  whose cables were cut), as we believe; no page read says so. The sinking was 36 years ago on Wednesday, not a round
  date, so any slot will do. Kept out: the politicians quoted at the opening, the lawsuit, the contractor's dispute
  over the blame, faces, logos. Not in LINEUP: the main chat's call.
  [KUOW, Mar 28 2026](https://www.kuow.org/stories/sound-transit-links-seattle-and-the-eastside-opening-day-on-the-crosslake-connection),
  [FOX 13, Mar 28 2026](https://www.fox13seattle.com/news/seattle-train-floating-bridge),
  [UW CEE, May 18 2026](https://www.ce.washington.edu/news/article/2026-05-18/keeping-watch-one-kind-bridge),
  [UW thesis, 2013](https://digital.lib.washington.edu/researchworks/items/a6855025-3e74-4810-9f94-67cd2d5faae6),
  [Metro Magazine, Apr 2018](https://www.metro-magazine.com/10007312/seattles-floating-bridge-is-high-tech-light-rail-link),
  [History.com](https://www.history.com/this-day-in-history/november-25/lacey-v-murrow-memorial-bridge-sinks-to-the-bottom-of-lake-washington),
  [HistoryLink](https://www.historylink.org/file/2002),
  [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacey_V._Murrow_Memorial_Bridge)

- Gulf Construction Online (Atkins' design account, written before completion): the towers amplify "the wind speed at
  the turbine location by up to 30 per cent", bending the breeze into "an S-shaped streamline" that holds "within a
  45-degree wind azimuth, either side of the central axis"; "Wind Tunnel Testing was done by BMT and Atkins"; the
  turbines can work for winds "between 270 and 360 degrees" (at first "285 and 345"), and outside that range "the
  turbine will automatically adopt a 'standstill' mode". Brakes: the blades stall so "the power output stabilises",
  "the tip of the blade extends by centrifugal force and rotates to act as a self-regulating governor brake", and if
  the controls fail "the tip brake working in conjunction with the hydraulic brake" stops it. Bridges: "maintenance-free
  bearings where they connect to the buildings to allow the towers to move 0.5 m"; for vibration "the facility to add
  spoilers to the bridge and to adjust the tuned mass damper"; "blade clearance to the bridge of 1.12 m" ("a factor of
  1.35 safety margin"), watched by "a laser blade position monitoring system" that sets the turbine to standstill; "199
  different load cases" per turbine. The premium was "less than three per cent of project value", where the page warns
  of "excessive cost (up to 30 per cent of the project value)".

  blades good for "80 m/s in any direction"; the rotors are "fixed rather than pole-mounted"; the bridges "span 32 m"

- A topic gap on TikTok (new to these notes: no Next Big line yet): Next Big's "trending on TikTok" page ("Updated
  November 20, 2026", a rolling 7-day index of searches) lists, among searches with high demand and few videos, "how
  bread factories speed up baking" (477K), "inside the future of robot learning" (418K) and "electricity power" (+84%,
  151K). The first one is an everyday-thing build with a real engineering story: the Chorleywood bread process, a
  candidate build (not in LINEUP, never built). It was "developed in 1961 by scientists here at Campden BRI (then
  known as the British Baking Industries Research Association (BBIRA))" (Campden BRI, Oct 25 2019); the dough is
  worked by high shear for "around three minutes" at about "11 Wh/kg dough" for standard UK bread flours (stronger
  flours "possibly 12-13 Wh/kg or even higher"), with "a partial vacuum" in the later mixing stages (pressure at the
  start "came in over 20 years later") and "a fast-acting oxidizing agent (Vitamin C/ascorbic acid) and a small
  proportion of hard fat or emulsifier", so the "lengthy fermentation times" of traditional bread give way to a dough
  developed "in a few minutes" and "under four hours from flour to sliced bread" (Wikipedia: "about three and a half
  hours"). It "permitted a much greater proportion of lower-protein domestic wheat to be used in the grist", and "As
  of 2011 ... 80% of bread made in the United Kingdom used the process" (Wikipedia; Campden: "more than 30
  countries"). The three developers Wikipedia names are real people: never shown. The health claims about CBP bread
  are campaigners' (Wikipedia attributes them): keep them out. Neither page gives the old method's hours, so no
  "from X hours to minutes" line until a page does.

- Build 112, the Sydney Harbour Bridge: the beats LINEUP asks to check (new to these notes: the Nov 26 17:00 entry has
  the cables in the rock, the meeting of the halves, the span, the steel, the 96 locomotives and free 1930 photos, but
  not the heat, the cranes, the rivets or the paint). Heat: "Arch can rise or fall 18 centimetres due to heating or
  cooling" (NSW Government, Minister for Roads, Dec 5 2024); "expansion of the steel structure on hot days can
  increase the height of the arch by 18 cm (7.1 in)" (Wikipedia). What it stands on: "four steel bearings consisting
  of large hinge pins and massive steel bases for support of the arches" (NSW State Heritage Register listing, whose
  pictures are "the copyright of Heritage NSW or respective copyright owners": not free). No page read ties the pins
  to the heat, so the script keeps two facts (the arch rises and falls; it stands on four giant pins), not a cause.
  The listing's "maximum thrust at the hinges" of "435,000,000 lb. per hinge" looks about ten times too big (by a
  rough sum of ours, some 20,000 tonnes a hinge): do not use it. Cranes: "two 580 tonne electrically operated creeper
  cranes" (Museums of History NSW guide), one "on each side of the harbour", and they "would "creep" along the arches
  as they were constructed, eventually meeting up in the middle" (Wikipedia); "The cranes hoisted men and materials
  into place", and came down when the road deck was finished in 1931 (ICE). Closing: "Workers riveted both top and
  bottom sections of the arch together, and the arch became self-supporting, allowing the support cables to be
  removed" (Wikipedia). The hour of the meeting differs ("at 10pm", MHNSW; "just before midnight", NSW Government):
  say "one night in August 1930". Rivets: "Approximately six million hand driven rivets and 52,800 tonnes of steel"
  (BridgeClimb); how they were heated and thrown is on none of the pages read, so a riveting shot can show the work
  but the script names no method. Ships: "Road deck is 49 metres above the water" (NSW Government).

  heat shimmer (a break); salt spray and orange rust spots on the grey steel (a break); two small robots blasting

New content
- Build 113, Formula Rossa: how the launch works and how the train stops (new to these notes: the Nov 26 21:00 entry
  has the record, the specs and the goggles, and found the braking on no page). The pages read describe Intamin's
  hydraulic launch in general, the type Wikipedia's "Accelerator Coaster" page lists Formula Rossa under, so the
  script says "a launch like this", not that each number is Formula Rossa's own. The store: pumps "push hydraulic
  fluid into several accumulators", each with "one side filled with hydraulic fluid and the other with nitrogen gas",
  and "It takes approximately 45 seconds to pressurize the accumulators with all pumps operating". The pull: "a large
  winch, around which the launch cables are wound", "driven by hydraulic turbines"; "The two launch cables are
  attached to the sides of the catch-car", which takes the train by a "launch dog" that "drops down from the center
  car" and is "held in place by a small magnet"; the catch car runs "underneath the coaster's train and track",
  "towing the train down a portion of the track at high speed, with it then detaching at the last moment" (How It
  Works). The stop: "magnetic brakes that smoothly stop the train without making contact", and "The catch-car is
  stopped by magnetic brakes identical to those used to stop the train" (Accelerator Coaster). LINEUP's carrier line
  has a source: a "hydraulic launch system similar in concept to those that fling fighter jets off aircraft carriers"
  (Popular Science, 2011, on Formula Rossa), with "F1 shaped trains", a track "inspired by" Monza, "1.3-mile" long,
  the ride "92 seconds". Fixes that fit the shot plan: nitrogen squeezed by the fluid like a giant spring (the spring
  is ours), the winch and cables pulling a catch car that lets go at full speed, magnets that brake without touching,
  the goggles against the sand. Shots: red race-car-shaped trains with no badge, name or horse (rule 8), riders only
  from behind.
  [Wikipedia, Accelerator Coaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accelerator_Coaster),

## 2026-11-27 18:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

New content
- 114's working parts, a first pass (new to these notes; the LINEUP line names CSMonitor and Hackaday only). The
  wall is "less than a tenth of a millimeter thick" (CSMonitor, Apr 14 2015), "progressively cut to less than 0.004
  in." from "a 5½-in.-diameter, 0.011-in.-thick blank" in "two drawing operations followed by three ironing passes
  through die rings", at "five can bodies/second" (Machine Design, Howard A. Kuhn, Dec 9 2009). The bottom is pressed
  "into a concave dome shape, which allows the can to withstand greater pressures than if it were flat" (CSMonitor).
  The neck: a necker "forms a 5-centimeter neck on the can. This is done gradually in 11 steps" (Penn State MATSE
  81's transcript of a How It's Made episode, with the "disc 14 centimeters in diameter" and "1800 cans per minute");
  Hackaday (Apr 17 2015) says Bill Hammack's video counts "the aluminum savings through reducing the top diameter of
  the can". The lid is a harder metal: alloy 5182, "stronger and harder to form than the can-body alloy" (3004). The
  liner is "a barrier between the drink and the aluminum so the drink won't end up tasting like metal" (Penn State).
  Each can must "withstand the vertical loads of 24 filled cans stacked above it during shipping", and recycling
  "slashes 95% of the energy needed to smelt and refine aluminum from natural ores" (Machine Design). CSMonitor's
  "The whole process takes only a seventh of a second" does not say which step: check the video before a line uses it.
  [CSMonitor, Apr 14 2015](https://csmonitor.com/Science/Science-Notebook/2015/0414/The-surprising-science-behind-the-aluminum-soda-can),
  [Machine Design, Dec 9 2009](https://machinedesign.com/materials/metals/article/21832055/aluminum-cans-a-lesson-in-product-development),
  [Penn State MATSE 81](https://courses.ems.psu.edu/matse81/book/export/html/2216),
  [Hackaday, Apr 17 2015](https://hackaday.com/2015/04/17/you-betta-recognize-the-aluminum-beverage-can)
- 114's pressure line needs care (LINEUP: "about three times a car tyre's pressure"). CSMonitor gives "up to 90
  pounds per square inch (about six times regular atmospheric pressure)", but Machine Design says "They normally
  exert 45 psi, 90 psi during pasteurization, and 110 psi in the trunk of a car in summer". So "three times a tyre"
  is the peak, not the usual can, and no source here gives the tyre figure: script "up to 90 psi" or "up to six times
  the air around you".
- 114's tab, for a history or tease line (Smithsonian): Ermal "Ernie" Fraze of Dayton, Ohio made the pull-off
  "zip-top" after a 1959 picnic ("It was detached from cans and thrown away") and sold it to Alcoa, which used it on
  Iron City Beer cans; the tabs "littered the streets and sidewalks and beaches" and brought "cuts and ingestion"; the
  "push-in-fold-back tab we use today" was patented in 1976 by "Daniel F. Cudzik, an engineer with Reynolds Metal
  Co." Real people and brands: names in the script at most, never shown, and no brand on any can.
  [Smithsonian](https://www.smithsonianmag.com/innovation/how-popping-open-a-can-became-the-sound-of-summer-180982527/)
- Leads for 114's first-aluminium-can line (search titles only, not read): Waste360's "MillerCoors Celebrates 60th

  rope anchorages to connect at bottom chord level", "following which the required load was jacked into the top
  chord to make the arch two-pinned", and "The temporary wire rope anchorages were then removed and the creeper
  cranes returned down the arch"; a photo caption there reads "Six Panels to close, three on each side, gap only 360
  feet -Photo taken 20.6.1930". The hour again: "On 19 August 1930 at 10pm" (MHNSW) and "at 10 pm on 19 August 1930"
  (the myplace education page, which adds "during the next nine months, steel decking was hung from the arch"). A
  figure to settle before a line uses it: the same Melbourne page gives "a creeper crane with a lifting capacity of
  122 tonnes" where MHNSW's guide has "two 580 tonne electrically operated creeper cranes" (perhaps weight against
  lift: no page read says). Still on no page read: how far the cables were let out each day, any trouble with wind or

## 2026-11-27 22:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

New content
- A candidate build, the QR code (an everyday thing with a real story; new to LINEUP and these notes): "The QR code
  was introduced in 1994 by Japanese automotive manufacturer Denso" to "speed up its tracking of car parts", because
  scanning several barcodes on each box was "slowing the production and distribution process" (IEEE Spectrum, Nov 13
  2020); the team was "led by Masahiro Hara" at Denso Wave (EPO, a 2014 European Inventor Award finalist). Two
  pictures for the hook and a fix: the game of Go, "Even if the pieces are a little off the intersection, the players
  still know where the pieces are" (so lost dots can be made up by the others), and a view from his window, "a tall
  building standing out from its surroundings", for a mark that would "stand out from the surrounding text" (IEEE).
  The three corner squares: "A scan through its centre produces bands in the proportion 1:1:3:1:1, a ratio Denso says
  its engineers chose after analysing printed material" (Make Tech Easier; IEEE: about three months of scanning
  newspaper pictures to find it); they "allow the code to be read quickly and from any angle" (EPO). Capacity: a
  barcode holds "up to 12 numbers" (IEEE; EPO "about 20 alphanumeric characters"), the QR code "numbers of up to 7,087
  digits" (Hara in IEEE; EPO "about 7 000 numerals": script "about 7,000 digits"). Speed differs: "five times faster
  than a typical barcode" (IEEE), "20 times faster than previous matrix codes" (EPO): script "five times faster". The
  hook line: four levels, "L, M, Q, and H", restore "7, 15, 25, and 30 per cent of total codewords" (Reed-Solomon,
  from a 1960 paper), so it is "built to survive being torn, stained, or partly scribbled over", but the 30 % "is not
  a guarantee that any randomly chosen 30 per cent of the visible square can be covered" (Make Tech Easier): script
  "up to about a third of it can be damaged". The open patent: Denso Wave "made the specification public and waived
  enforcement of its patent for standardised QR codes" (Make Tech Easier); patents granted in the US in 1998, Japan
  1999 and Europe 2000 (IEEE); an ISO standard in 2000 (Make Tech Easier; IEEE's "ISO standard 18004:2015 in 2000"
  mixes up the edition). Proof: "Many restaurants are displaying QR codes that allow customers to pull up the
  eatery's menu on their phone" (IEEE, Nov 2020; no figure). A free proof shot: US5726435, "Optically readable
  two-dimensional code and method and apparatus using the same" (inventors Masahiro Hara, Motoaki Watabe, Tadao
  Nojiri, Takayuki Nagaya, Yuji Uchiyama; assignees NipponDenso and Toyota Central R&D Labs), whose Fig. 1 is a
  21 x 21 cell code with its three positioning squares; no copyright notice seen in the part read (the USPTO rule of
  the Nov 23 22:56 entry; its dates were not read). Script "for car factories", not "Toyota invented it" (Toyota's
  part is a co-inventor's lab).
  [IEEE Spectrum, Nov 13 2020](https://spectrum.ieee.org/how-a-board-game-and-skyscrapers-inspired-the-development-of-the-qr-code),

- A candidate build, the Millau Viaduct in southern France (a famous mega-structure in Europe; new to LINEUP and these
  notes; it opened to traffic on Dec 16 2004, so its 22nd birthday falls on Dec 16 2026). Hook: a road above the
  clouds, "53 ft taller than the Eiffel Tower" at its tallest point and "the world's tallest traffic bridge" (Roads &
  Bridges, Jul 2005); "the tallest bridge in the world, having a structural height of 336.4 meters" (Structurae,
  quoting Wikipedia), while its table gives "max. 343 m" above ground (R&B: 1,125 ft): script "taller than the Eiffel
  Tower" and no figure for the top. The deck is 270 m above the valley floor, a height Chinese suspension bridges have
  since passed, so "tallest bridge", never "highest road". Problem: "the wide valley of the Tarn River near the city
  of Millau" and "heavy congestion at the beginning and end of the July and August holiday season" (Structurae) on
  the A75 between Paris and Barcelona (R&B). Fixes: seven piers, each on "four deep shafts", the tallest (P2)
  244.96 m, each growing "4 meters" every three days; every pier splits in two near the top ("This bisectioning of the
  box provides for the required flexibility") so the 2,460 m deck can grow and shrink with the heat; the
  36,000-tonne steel deck was pushed out from both ends at once over temporary towers (eight, Structurae; seven, R&B):
  "Each push cycle moved the deck 600 mm and took four minutes", on lifting cylinders "with a capacity of 250 tons",
  steered by GPS (R&B; Structurae describes laser guidance troubled by wind and mist); the two halves met on 28 May
  2004 (Structurae). Wind: "wind shields are installed which prevent the overturning of high-sided vehicles", the deck
  has an "inverse airfoil shape, providing negative lift", reshaped after wind-tunnel tests, built "for winds of up to
  225 kilometers per hour" (Structurae). Masts: seven, each 87 m and about 700 tonnes, "tilted upwards, as one
  piece", each with eleven pairs of stays (7 x 22 = 154). Proof: a load test of "920 tons" before it opened to
  traffic on 16 December 2004 at 09:00; 38 months of works; tolls "for 75 years, until 2080" (Structurae). The cost
  figures clash (€ 300 million in the table, "about € 400 million" and "approximately € 394 million" in the text,
  R&B "$523 million"): no cost line. France's president inaugurated it on Dec 14 2004: keep him out (no real faces).
  Wikipedia could not be read from here.

  steel tendons" in the core (Wikipedia). Break, the skin: on the steel diagrid "none of the panels is coplanar to

- A candidate build, the launch pad that floods itself at liftoff (new to LINEUP and these notes; a build on the
  ground, so it keeps LINEUP's rule against far-away space pictures, and NASA's real launch film can be B5). The
  break, the rocket's own roar: SLS "will create about 176 decibels at liftoff", and the pressure waves "can cause
  vibrations that could potentially damage the mobile launcher, as well as the rocket" (NASA, Sep 16 2019, upd. Jul
  26 2023). The fix, Pad 39B's Ignition Overpressure Protection and Sound Suppression system: "approximately 400,000
  gallons" at liftoff "from large overhead holding tanks" (NASA blog, Jul 19 2024, upd. Sep 29 2025); the 2019 test
  poured "About 450,000 gallons" in "the 30-second test", at a peak of "more than 1 million gallons per minute". The
  totals clash: script "about 400,000 gallons" (the Artemis II page) or no total. How the water gets there: "mobile
  launcher interface pipes", risers and nozzles at the top of the flame deflector (NASA feature, undated);
  "redesigned rainbird water nozzles" and new "water balancing plates" for Artemis II (2024). It only knocks the noise
  "down a few decibels" (2019): no percent in a script. Where it goes: "east and west holding ponds through channels,
  called water flumes" (undated feature). With no launcher on the pad, a test shot water "about 100 feet in the air"
  (undated feature): the A1 hook picture. Thrust: "8.8 million pounds of thrust" (NASA, Nov 23 2022; Spaceflight Now,
  Dec 9 2022), so the undated page's "a combined 8.4 million pounds" is out. The heat: the flame trench is lined with
  about 96,000 heat-resistant bricks in three sizes, rated to "temperatures of up to 2,000 degrees Fahrenheit", its
  north side about 571 ft long, 58 ft wide and 42 ft high, and the new deflector throws the plume north (NASA, May 11
  2017). The real break: the first launch, Artemis I on Nov 16 2022, "blew the doors off elevators that run up and
  down the structure", which "wasn't expected" (Spaceflight Now); "The most significant issue is the damage to the
  elevators", so crews walked the tower's 662 steps to inspect it (NASA, Nov 23 2022, upd. Sep 29 2023). Before a
  crew flew: "new water nozzles" for the sound suppression (Spaceflight Now; the 2024 page's rainbird nozzles); neither
  page says the elevator doors were made stronger, so no line says how they were fixed. Chain: the roar shakes the
  rocket (A2) → flood the pad (A3) → a 2,000 °F blast at the pad's floor (A4) → 96,000 heat bricks and a deflector
  that throws it north (A5) → the first launch blew the doors off the tower's elevators (A6) → repairs and new
  nozzles before a crew flew (B1) → half a million gallons with nowhere to go (B2) → the flumes to the ponds (B3) →
  B5 the real Artemis II launch of Apr 1 2026. A1: a white geyser bursting 100 ft over an empty concrete launch pad
  at dawn. B6: funny slow motion, a backyard sprinkler switched on mid-party soaks the guests. Pad and rocket only:
  no NASA logos, no crew.

## 2026-11-28 02:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

New content
- A candidate build, Abu Simbel, the temple that was cut up and carried up a hill (new to LINEUP and these notes as a
  build of its own; the Aswan build's line 11 only names it). Egypt, so an Arab-world story for Kef Tenbeni, and a
  famous place with a real rescue. The break: the High Dam's Lake Nasser "would reach full capacity in 1966"
  (National Geographic). The fix: "More than a thousand blocks, each weighing some 30 tons" (UNESCO; Atlas Copco
  "between 20 and 30 tons"; JSTOR Daily's "Over 7,000 other blocks" clashes, so script "more than a thousand
  blocks"). The stone fights back: it is porous sandstone, so "explosives could not be used to any greater degree"
  and the walls were cut with handsaws "due to power saws causing too much waste" (Atlas Copco); "workers using wire
  saws started slicing them up" in November 1963 (UNESCO); "copious volumes" of epoxy went into the sandstone "to
  keep it from crumbling" (JSTOR Daily). The rising water: "A temporary dam was built around the site to keep it
  dry" (NatGeo). The new home: "a specially built artificial cliff, 64 m above the old site and 180 m inland"
  (UNESCO; Atlas Copco "about 200 meters further inland and 65 meters higher": script "about 65 metres higher"),
  the halls rebuilt "under an artificial hill supported by concrete domes" (JSTOR Daily), "About 330,000 cubic
  meters of stone" placed over the cupolas, and "The permitted deviation was just plus or minus 5 mm" (Atlas Copco).
  B5: "inaugurated in its new location on 22 September 1968" (UNESCO), set so the sun still lights the inner halls
  "during certain hours of the spring and autumn" (Atlas Copco): "Twice a year, on Oct. 22 and Feb. 22", a beam that
  "traveled about 60 meters (200 feet) into the temple" and "lasts about 20 minutes", and "Engineers took care to
  preserve the temple's alignment with the sun" (AP, Oct 22 2026); no page says the move shifted the days, so no line
  says so. Peg: post it near Feb 22 2027. Money and people: Atlas Copco's "SEK 200 million" is this
  project, UNESCO's US$80 million the whole Nubia campaign, and JSTOR's 25,000 workers are the dam's: no worker count.
  Chain: the lake rising (A2) → cut it into blocks (A3) → no blasting in soft sandstone (A4) → handsaws, wire saws and
  epoxy (A5) → the lake keeps rising while they cut (A6) → a temporary dam (B1) → a temple carved into a cliff, and
  the new site has no cliff (B2) → an artificial hill on concrete domes, every block back within 5 mm (B3). A1: a
  giant carved stone face as big as a truck hanging from a crane hook above the desert river. B6: funny slow motion,
  a flat-pack wardrobe finished with one screw left over. The statues only as the landmark: no made-up pharaoh, no
  gods or rituals in the lines (LINEUP's rule), 1960s workers from behind or hands only.
  [UNESCO, Abu Simbel](https://whc.unesco.org/en/story-abu-simbel),
  [National Geographic, Jul 17 2019](https://www.nationalgeographic.com/history/world-history-magazine/article/egyptian-temples-excavation-abu-simbel),
  [Atlas Copco Egypt, Abu Simbel](https://www.atlascopco.com/en-eg/history-in-egypt/landmark-projects-abu-simbel),
  [JSTOR Daily, Feb 21 2025](https://daily.jstor.org/an-epic-face-lift-moving-abu-simbel-out-of-the-nile),
  [AP via WCAX, Oct 22 2026](https://www.wcax.com/2026/10/22/twice-year-golden-sunbeam-illuminates-3000-year-old-egyptian-temple/)

## 2026-11-28 03:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

New content
- A candidate build for Kef Tenbeni, Dubai's Cayan Tower, the skyscraper that turns a quarter turn on its way up
  (new to LINEUP and these notes; a Dubai Marina landmark with a real construction story). It "twists 90 degrees"
  (Wikipedia, SOM, MEED) over 306.4 m and 73 floors above ground, 5 below (CTBUH, "All-Concrete", begun 2006; SOM
  and ME Construction News say 307 m, MEED 303 m, SOM 75 floors: go by CTBUH), opened 10 June 2013 (Wikipedia), SOM
  as architect and structural engineer. The trick: the floor plates, "each is slightly rotated against the story
  below it", turn around "a cylindrical concrete pillar at the center" (SOM), each floor "set 1.2 degrees clockwise
  from the floor below" (CTBUH; Wikipedia 1.2 too, MEED "about one degree", MECN 1.08: script "1.2 degrees a
  floor"), so "each of the 48 columns moves in different directions" (MECN). Why turn it: "Wind load and solar heat gain are reduced compared to a rectilinear building of
  the same height", and more tenants get "desirable views of the nearby marina and gulf" (SOM, which also names
  wind-tunnel tests, staggered metal screen panels and deep window sills against the sun). The break that makes the
  story: on 7 February 2007 (Wikipedia) the diaphragm wall failed after cracks in "the secant piles" and "water from
  the marina poured in to the basement", a "20-metre-deep" pit (MEED; Snopes "20 meters below ground level"; MECN's
  "27m" is the excavation), with 39 on site and none hurt (MEED; Snopes quotes Gulf News' "Nearly 100 workers": no
  head count in a script); Snopes rates the flooded-site photos "True". Work stood still "a year and a half" and
  resumed in July 2008 (Wikipedia; MECN: the restoration took "14 months" and about 30% of the budget). Its twist
  record went to the Shanghai Tower in February 2015 (Wikipedia), so no "most twisted" line. Chain: a plain box tower
  takes the full wind and sun (A2) → turn every floor 1.2 degrees round a concrete core (A3) → the columns can no
  longer stand straight (A4) → columns that step a little at every floor (A5) → the Marina breaks into the 20 m pit
  (A6) → pump it dry, rebuild the wall, restart in 2008 (B1) → desert sun on the glass (B2) → staggered metal screens
  and deep sills (B3). A1: a glass tower turning a quarter turn above a marina at dusk, slow aerial climb. B6: funny
  slow motion, someone wringing out a soaked bath towel, water everywhere (ours). No "Infinity", developer or builder
  names in the prompts, no logos, workers only from behind. Free photos: not found yet (a Commons file page turned up
  in search under a wrong host and could not be read from here).
  [CTBUH Skyscraper Center](https://www.skyscrapercenter.com/building/cayan-tower/464),

  [MEED, undated](https://www.meed.com/work-to-restart-on-flood-hit-infinity-tower/),
  [Snopes, upd. Jun 7 2007](https://www.snopes.com/fact-check/infinity-tower/),

## 2026-11-28 04:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

New content
- A candidate build for both channels, Japan's Seikan Tunnel, the railway under the sea between Honshu and Hokkaido
  (new to LINEUP and these notes). 53.85 km long (Wikipedia, web-japan, Guinness; Britannica 53.8), 23.3 km of it
  under the seabed, the track "240 metres (790 ft) below sea level" and "100 metres (330 ft) below the seabed"
  (Wikipedia); Guinness lists it as the "Lowest railway line" ("a depth of 240m 786ft below sea level"). Why: in a
  1954 typhoon five ferries sank in the Tsugaru Strait, "killing 1,430 passengers" (Wikipedia, PBS; one was the Toya
  Maru), and a bridge was ruled out for the "unpredictable weather conditions" (PBS). How: three bores, a pilot, a
  service and a main tunnel, joined by drifts "at 600-1,000-metre intervals"; volcanic rock on the Honshu side,
  sedimentary on the Hokkaido side, crushed by "Igneous intrusions and faults"; the boring machine was abandoned
  "after less than 2 kilometres" for "difficulty in accessing the face for advanced grouting" (all Wikipedia; PBS
  says they "couldn't use a tunnel boring machine": script "the machine gave up"). The flood: in 1976 water "gushed
  into the tunnel at a whopping rate of 80 tons per minute" and "It took more than two months to control" (PBS
  only; a 1977 TRID abstract has 14 cubic metres a minute at 16.89 km, maybe another inflow). Dates: excavation from
  1964 (Wikipedia, Britannica; web-japan "September 1971", the main works), pilot breakthrough 27 January 1983, main
  bore-through 10 March 1985, opened 13 March 1988; "34 workers were killed during construction" (Wikipedia,
  Britannica: a fact for the record, not a dramatized shot). Today: Hokkaido Shinkansen since March 2016 on a
  three-rail dual-gauge track (1,435 and 1,067 mm) shared with freight; "speeds are limited to 160 km/h" inside, and
  on holidays, when "freight traffic is suspended", it runs at 260 km/h (Wikipedia, citing a 2024 Asahi report on the first 260 km/h
  run in Golden Week). Its two undersea stations, Tappi-Kaitei and Yoshioka-Kaitei, "closed with the construction of
  the Hokkaido Shinkansen" (Wikipedia). Say "the deepest railway on Earth", not "the longest undersea tunnel": the
  Channel Tunnel's undersea part is longer (37.9 km) and Gotthard is longer overall. Chain: ferries sink in
  typhoons (A2) → dig three tunnels under the strait, the small pilot first (A3) → crushed rock lets the sea in, 80
  tons a minute in 1976 (A4) → grout pumped into the rock ahead of the face (A5) → the boring machine blocks the
  grouting and is abandoned (A6) → drill and blast, metre by metre, until the 1983 breakthrough (B1) → bullet trains
  and narrow-gauge freight must share one track (B2) → a third rail, 160 km/h, 260 on holidays (B3). A1: a bullet
  train racing through a lit tunnel, the camera rising through rock and dark seawater to a storm on the surface.
  B6: funny slow motion, someone seen from behind bailing a flooding sandcastle moat with a tiny bucket, wave after
  wave (ours). Workers only from behind, no disaster victims shown, no railway logos. Free pictures: the search found
  a Commons file "Seikan Tunnel 3d cross section 1a.svg" and a "Category:Seikan Tunnel", both under a wrong host
  (www.Wikipedia.org) that could not be read from here: check their licences on Commons before use.
  [Wikipedia, Seikan Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seikan_Tunnel),
  [PBS Building Big](https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/buildingbig/wonder/structure/seikan.html),
  [Guinness, Lowest railway line](https://www.guinnessworldrecords.com/world-records/62985-lowest-railway-line),
  [TRID, 1977](https://trid.trb.org/view/59179),
  [Britannica](https://www.britannica.com/topic/Seikan-Tunnel),
  [web-japan](https://web-japan.org/atlas/architecture/arc02.html)
